. To understand the behavior of the transfer matrix, we need to investigate its components. Each component of the transfer matrix will correspond to a microstate of rows
\mu
and
\mu+1
. Since there are
n
spin sites at any given row, and each spin takes two values, namely
\pm 1
, there are
2^{n}
microstates for each row. One possible basis to represent these microstates can be constructed by introducing a 2 component vector for each spin value. Let
i
denote one of these microstates and let
|s^{(i)}_{\nu}\rangle
denote the value of the
\nu
th spin value in the
i
th microstate. We assign,

\displaystyle s^{(i)}_{\nu}=+1\Rightarrow|s^{(i)}_{\nu}\rangle={\left|{+}\right>}=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}

(296)

\displaystyle s^{(i)}_{\nu}=-1\Rightarrow|s^{(i)}_{\nu}\rangle={\left|{-}\right>}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}

(297)

Then, each microstate
i
can be expressed as follows,

{\left|{i}\right>}=\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{n}|s^{(i)}_{\nu}\rangle

(298)

Where
i=1\dots 2^{n}
. Here, each basis vector is the standard basis vector in
\mathbb{R}^{2^{n}}
. For example,

{\left|{1}\right>}=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\\
\vdots\\
0\end{pmatrix}=\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{n}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}

Corresponds to a state where each spin value in the row is
+1
. This way of expressing the microstate is equivalent to representing the state as a number in base 2, where +1 corresponds to 0 and -1 corresponds to 1. Then, the sum over all possible microstates in the partition function simply becomes a sum over
i
for each row
\mu
. We can rewrite the partition function as follows,

Z=\sum_{i_{1}\dots i_{m}}\prod_{\mu=1}^{m}\left[\left(\prod_{\nu=1}^{n}\exp(bs^{(i_{\mu})}_{\nu}s_{\nu}^{(i_{\mu+1})})\right)\left(\prod_{\nu=1}^{n}\delta_{i_{\mu},i_{\mu+1}}\exp(as_{\nu}^{(i_{\mu})}s_{\nu+1}^{(i_{\mu+1})})\right)\right]

(299)

This is completely identical with eq.
295
. At this point, let us introduce two intermediary matrices
V^{a}
and
V^{b}
by their components in the following way,

\displaystyle\begin{cases}V^{a}_{ij}=\prod_{\nu}\delta_{ij}\exp(as_{\nu}^{(i)}s_{\nu+1}^{(j)})={\left<{i}\right|}V^{a}{\left|{j}\right>}\\
\\
V^{b}_{ij}=\prod_{\nu}\exp(bs^{(i)}_{\nu}s_{\nu}^{(j)})={\left<{i}\right|}V^{b}{\left|{j}\right>}\end{cases}

(300)

Then the partition function is,

Z=\sum_{i_{1}\dots i_{m}}\prod_{\mu=1}^{m}V^{a}_{i_{1}i_{2}}V^{a}_{i_{2}i_{3}}\dots V^{a}_{i_{m}i_{1}}V^{b}_{i_{1}i_{2}}V^{b}_{i_{2}i_{3}}\dots V^{b}_{i_{m}i_{1}}

Here again, we used the fact that we have cylindrical boundary conditions. Thus
i_{m+1}=i_{1}
. They are in the same microstate. If we define
T=V^{a}V^{b}
and calculate its components, we get,

T_{ij}=\sum_{k}V^{a}_{ik}V^{b}_{kj}=\sum_{k}\prod_{\nu}\exp(as^{(i)}_{\nu}s^{(k)}_{\nu})\exp(bs^{(k)}_{\nu}s^{(j)}_{\nu+1})\delta_{kj}=\prod_{\nu}\exp(as^{(i)}_{\nu}s^{(j)}_{\nu}+bs^{(j)}_{\nu}s^{(j)}_{\nu+1})

This is the exact expression given in
295
. Therefore,
T
is the transfer matrix. This also means that
T_{ij}=V^{a}_{ij}V^{b}_{ij}
. Hence, the partition function becomes,

Z=\sum_{i_{1}\dots i_{m}}\prod_{\mu=1}^{m}T_{i_{1}i_{2}}T_{i_{2}i_{3}}\dots T_{i_{m}i_{1}}=\sum_{i_{1}}T^{m}_{i_{1}i_{1}}=\tr T^{m}

(301)

It turns out that the matrices
V^{a}
and
V^{b}
can be expressed using the matrices
X_{\nu}
and
Z_{\nu}
which are defined in terms of the Pauli matrices and are given as follows,

X_{\nu}=\underbrace{\mathds{1}\otimes\cdots\mathds{1}\otimes\sigma_{x}}_{\nu\textrm{th place}}\otimes\mathds{1}\dots\otimes\mathds{1}\,,

(302)

And
Z_{\nu}
and
Y_{\nu}
are defined analogously. Here are the Pauli matrices,

\sigma_{x}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\sigma_{y}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-i\\
i&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\sigma_{z}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}

Along with the 2-dimensional identity matrix
\mathds{1}
, these matrices already form a representation for the 3-dimensional rotation group
SO(3)
. We will later discuss this in great detail but it turns out that the matrices
X_{\nu},Y_{\nu},Z_{\nu}
can be used to create the generators of
n
-dimensional rotation group. Defining
\bar{b}
by the equation
\sinh{2b}\sinh 2\bar{b}=1
with
\bar{b}>0
, we can also express
V^{a}
and
V^{b}
as,

\displaystyle\begin{cases}V^{b}=(2\sinh{2b})^{n/2}\exp({\bar{b}\sum_{\nu=1}^{n}X_{\nu}})\\
\\
V^{a}=\exp(a\sum_{\nu=1}^{n}Z_{\nu}Z_{\nu+1})\end{cases}

(303)

We define,

\displaystyle B=\sum_{\nu=1}^{n}X_{\nu}

\displaystyle A=\sum_{\nu=1}^{n}Z_{\nu}Z_{\nu+1}

(304)

It is not immediately obvious why these matrices are the same as those defined in eq.
300
. To do that we have to investigate the components of
V^{a}
and
V^{b}
in the basis that we have constructed earlier.

6.1.1
Components of
V^{a}

The components of
V^{a}
are expressed as,

V^{a}_{ij}={\left<{i}\right|}V^{a}{\left|{j}\right>}={\left<{i}\right|}\prod_{\nu=1}^{n}\exp(aZ_{\nu}Z_{\nu+1}){\left|{j}\right>}

This must be equal to the expression in eq.
300
. To understand how this matrix acts on the basis vectors, we first have to see how
\sigma_{z}
acts on vectors
{\left|{+}\right>}
and
{\left|{-}\right>}
, representing the state of one spin site. Notice,

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\sigma_{z}{\left|{+}\right>}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}={\left|{+}\right>}\\
\\
\sigma_{z}{\left|{-}\right>}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
-1\end{pmatrix}=-{\left|{-}\right>}\end{cases}

(305)

Or, more concisely,

\sigma_{z}|s^{(i)}_{\nu}\rangle=s^{(i)}_{\nu}|s^{(i)}_{\nu}\rangle

(306)

Then it follows that,

Z_{\nu}{\left|{i}\right>}=\left[\bigotimes_{k=1}^{\nu-1}\mathds{1}\otimes\sigma_{z}\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{k=\nu+1}^{n}\mathds{1}\right)\right]\bigotimes_{\lambda=1}^{n}|s^{(i)}_{\lambda}\rangle=\bigotimes_{\lambda=1}^{\nu-1}|s^{(i)}_{\lambda}\rangle\otimes\sigma_{z}|s^{(i)}_{\nu}\rangle\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{\lambda=\nu+1}^{n}|s^{(i)}_{\lambda}\rangle\right)

=\bigotimes_{\lambda=1}^{\nu-1}|s^{(i)}_{\lambda}\rangle\otimes s^{(i)}_{\nu}|s^{(i)}_{\nu}\rangle\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{\lambda=\nu+1}^{n}|s^{(i)}_{\lambda}\rangle\right)=s^{(i)}_{\nu}{\left|{i}\right>}

Hence, it turns out that
|i\rangle
is an eigenvector of the matrix
Z_{\nu}
with the eigenvalue
s^{(i)}_{\nu}
.
Z_{\nu}
is diagonalized in this basis. Then,

Z_{\nu}Z_{\nu+1}{\left|{i}\right>}=s^{(i)}_{\nu}s^{(i)}_{\nu+1}{\left|{i}\right>}

The eigenvalue
s^{(i)}_{\nu}s^{(i)}_{\nu+1}
equals
+1
if
s^{(i)}_{\nu}
is equal to
s^{(i)}_{\nu+1}
and equals
-1
if they have the opposite sign. It ”checks” whether two adjacent spin sites have the same spin value. Since
{\left|{i}\right>}
is an eigenvector of
Z_{\nu}Z_{\nu+1}
then it is also an eigenvector of a
\exp(bZ_{\nu}Z_{\nu+1})
with the eigenvalue
\exp(as^{(i)}_{\nu}s^{(i)}_{\nu+1})
. Which indicates,

{\left<{i}\right|}V^{a}{\left|{j}\right>}={\left<{i}\right|}\prod_{\nu=1}^{n}\exp(aZ_{\nu}Z_{\nu+1}){\left|{j}\right>}=\prod_{\nu=1}^{n}\exp(as^{(i)}_{\nu}s^{(i)}_{\nu+1}){\left<{i}\right|}{\left|{j}\right>}=\prod_{\nu=1}^{n}\exp(as^{(i)}_{\nu}s^{(i)}_{\nu+1})\delta_{ij}

(307)

Which is exactly what we needed to show.

6.1.2
Components of
V^{b}

Components of
V^{b}
can be found as follows,

V^{b}_{ij}=(2\sinh 2b)^{n/2}{\left<{i}\right|}\prod_{\nu}^{n}\exp(\bar{b}X_{\nu}){\left|{j}\right>}

And again, this must be equal to the expression given in eq.
300
. Similar to the calculation in the previous part, we begin by denoting what
\sigma_{x}|s^{(i)}_{\nu}\rangle
is,

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\sigma_{x}{\left|{+}\right>}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}={\left|{-}\right>}\\
\\
\sigma_{x}{\left|{-}\right>}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}={\left|{+}\right>}\end{cases}

Or, in short,

\sigma_{x}|s^{(i)}_{\nu}\rangle=|-s^{(i)}_{\nu}\rangle

Then we have,

X_{\nu}|i\rangle=\left[\bigotimes_{k=1}^{\nu-1}\mathds{1}\otimes\sigma_{x}\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{k=\nu+1}^{n}\mathds{1}\right)\right]\bigotimes_{\lambda=1}^{n}|s^{(i)}_{\lambda}\rangle=\bigotimes_{\lambda=1}^{\nu-1}|s^{(i)}_{\lambda}\rangle\otimes\sigma_{x}|s^{(i)}_{\nu}\rangle\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{\lambda=\nu+1}^{n}|s^{(i)}_{\lambda}\rangle\right)

=\bigotimes_{\lambda=1}^{\nu-1}|s^{(i)}_{\lambda}\rangle\otimes|-s^{(i)}_{\nu}\rangle\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{\lambda=\nu+1}^{n}|s^{(i)}_{\lambda}\rangle\right)

The result is another microstate of the row where the
\nu
th spin has the opposite sign. This vector also corresponds to another basis vector in the
{\left|{i}\right>}
basis. Effectively, the matrix
X_{\nu}
”flips” the
\nu
th spinsite of
{\left|{i}\right>}
and produces another basis vector within all possible microstates of the row. We can imagine this as a type of permutation
P_{\nu}:\{1,2,\dots 2^{n}\}\to\{1,2,\dots 2^{n}\}
with,

X_{\nu}{\left|{i}\right>}=|P_{\nu}(i)\rangle

Now we can investigate
\exp(\bar{b}X_{\nu})
. Let us expand this matrix,

\exp(\bar{b}X_{\nu})=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\dfrac{(\bar{b}X_{\nu})^{k}}{k!}

Where we have
X_{\nu}^{2}=\mathds{1}
. Evidently, we get,

\exp(\bar{b}X_{\nu})=\cosh\bar{b}\mathds{1}+\sinh\bar{b}X_{\nu}=\cosh\bar{b}(\mathds{1}+\tanh\bar{b}X_{\nu})

Notice that we have the following identities from the definition of
\bar{b}
, which will help us simplify these results.

\tanh b=e^{-2\bar{b}}\quad;\quad\tanh\bar{b}=e^{-2b}

\prod_{\nu=1}^{n}\exp(\bar{b}X_{\nu})=\cosh^{n}\bar{b}\prod_{\nu=1}^{n}(\mathds{1}+e^{-2b}X_{\nu})

Then,

V^{b}=(2\sinh{2b})^{n/2}\cosh^{n}\bar{b}\prod_{\nu=1}^{n}(\mathds{1}+e^{-2b}X_{\nu})

If we consider the coefficient of this expression, we see that,

\sqrt{2\sinh 2b}\cosh^{n}\bar{b}=\sqrt{\dfrac{2}{\sinh 2\bar{b}}}\cosh\bar{b}=\sqrt{\dfrac{\cosh^{2}\bar{b}}{\sinh\bar{b}\cosh\bar{b}}}=\sqrt{\dfrac{1}{\tanh\bar{b}}}=e^{b}

Finally, we arrive at the final expression for
V^{b}
, which is,

V^{b}=e^{nb}\prod_{\nu=1}^{n}(\mathds{1}+e^{-2b}X_{\nu})=e^{nb}\left(\mathds{1}+e^{-2b}\sum_{\nu=1}^{n}X_{\nu}+e^{-4b}\sum_{\nu_{1}<\nu_{2}}X_{\nu_{1}}X_{\nu_{2}}+\dots e^{-2nb}\prod_{\nu=1}^{n}X_{\nu}\right)

(308)

Its components are,

{\left<{i}\right|}V^{b}{\left|{j}\right>}=e^{nb}\left(\delta_{ij}+e^{-2b}\sum_{\nu}\delta_{i,P_{\nu}(j)}+\dots e^{-2nb}\delta_{i,P_{1}\dots P_{n}(j)}\right)

At this point, we can see how this matrix compares the energy of interaction between two adjacent rows. If the microstates
i
and
j
of these rows are the same, then the energy is
e^{nb}
, and from the Kronecker deltas in this expression, only the first term survives. However, if only one spin site has the opposite sign between
i
and
j
, then the difference in energy between the first case will be
e^{-2b}
. The coefficient of the second term reflects this. Accordingly, only one of the Kronecker deltas will survive from the second term. The same is true for any number of different spin sites between
i
and
j
. Kronecker deltas will pick the necessary combinations of
P_{\nu}
to get from
j
to
i
, and the coefficient of that term will adjust the energy in the exponent accordingly. This way,
V^{b}
accounts for all possible energies between different rows.

Up to this point, we introduced the physical background of the problem of calculating the partition function for the two-dimensional Ising model. We transformed the problem into calculating the trace of the transfer matrix and how to express the transfer matrix in terms of the generators of rotation groups. Now we investigate the problem from a representation theory perspective to find the eigenvalues of the transfer matrix.

6.2
Spin Representations

Before moving on with doing spinor analysis of the transfer matrix, we should examine the relations between
2n
and
2^{n}
dimensional spaces. This section will construct the spin representations of
2n
-dimensional rotation groups using a set of
2n
anti-commuting matrices.

Spin representations are double covers of
O(n)
or
SO(n)
groups with arbitrary dimension. In the physical sense, spin representations take the ”number of full turns before a rotation” into consideration. As clear from their names, we will see that a
2\pi
rotation would be equivalent to the initial state. Thus, contrary to ”normal” representations, every element can be represented with 2 distinct elements of their spin representations.

We start by writing a set of
2n
matrices
\Gamma_{k}
that anti-commute, which we will use to construct the
2^{n}
dimensional matrix algebra.

\displaystyle\{\Gamma_{k},\Gamma_{l}\}=2\delta_{kl}

(309)

To construct such quantities, we will use the faithful representations of the quaternion group as building blocks, with the smallest one being
2
-dimensional. The reader may refer to
[
24
]
for the physical interpretation of this construction. Which representation we will use is redundant. We may refer to the representations as
\{\mathds{1},\sigma_{x},\sigma_{y},\sigma_{z}\}
. Let us realize this set as the Pauli spin matrices for convenience since they already form a set of anti-commuting matrices. given,

X_{\nu}=\underbrace{\mathds{1}\otimes\cdots\mathds{1}\otimes\sigma_{x}}_{\textrm{$\nu$th place}}\otimes\mathds{1}\dots\otimes\mathds{1}\,,

(310)

and similarly,
Y_{\nu}
and
Z_{\nu}
. Now, we may construct a set of
2n
anti-commuting matrices
\Gamma_{k}
as

\displaystyle\Gamma_{2r-1}=X_{1}\cdots X_{r-1}\cdot Z_{r}\cdot\mathds{1}_{r+1}\cdot\mathds{1}_{n}

\displaystyle\Gamma_{2r}=X_{1}\cdots X_{r-1}\cdot Y_{r}\cdot\mathds{1}_{r+1}\cdot\mathds{1}_{n}\,.

(311)

Note that
\Gamma_{k}
are of the form of tensor product of
n

2\times 2
matrices, thus are of dimension
2^{n}
. Now, let us take all the possible products of these matrices. Since these matrices are anti-commuting, we know that
\Gamma_{k}^{2}=1
and changing the order of
\Gamma_{k}
matrices with different indices only adds a factor of
(-1)
, that is
\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}=-\Gamma_{l}\Gamma_{k}
for
k\neq l
. Thus we can see that
\Gamma
matrices can only appear in first power and changing the order does not result in independent matrices, leading to only
2^{2n}
independent matrices, which are of the form

\displaystyle 1,\,\Gamma_{1},\,\Gamma_{2},\,\cdots,\,\Gamma_{1}\Gamma_{2},\,\cdots,\,\Gamma_{1}\Gamma_{2}\Gamma_{3},\,\cdots,\,\Gamma_{1}\Gamma_{2}\Gamma_{3}\Gamma_{4},\,\cdots,\,\Gamma_{1}\Gamma_{2}\cdots\Gamma_{2n}\,.

Any
2^{n}
-dimensional matrix can be written as a linear combination of these
2^{2n}
independent matrices. Thus base matrices span the complete algebra of
2^{n}
dimensional matrices. For the proof, the reader may refer to
[
22
]
.

Now let us suppose we apply a similarity transformation to our
\Gamma_{k}
matrices to obtain a new converted set of
\Gamma_{k}^{*}
matrices,

\Gamma_{k}^{*}=S\Gamma_{k}S^{-1}\,.

(312)

Notice that the anti-commuting property is conserved by this transformation,

\displaystyle\{\Gamma_{k}^{*},\Gamma_{l}^{*}\}

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{k}^{*}\Gamma_{l}^{*}+\Gamma_{l}^{*}\Gamma_{k}^{*}

\displaystyle=S\Gamma_{k}S^{-1}S\Gamma_{l}S^{-1}+S\Gamma_{l}S^{-1}S\Gamma_{k}S^{-1}

\displaystyle=S(\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}+\Gamma_{l}\Gamma_{k})S^{-1}=2\delta_{kl}\,.

(313)

Hence, we can obtain other sets of anti-commuting matrices by applying similarity transformations to a set of anti-commuting matrices. These matrices are equivalently as good as other ones to obtain bases for
2^{n}
-dimensional matrices.

We have said that
\Gamma_{i}
matrices constitute a basis for the
2^{n}
-dimensional matrix algebra, and every automorphism of a matrix algebra is inner. Thus we know that
every
automorphism can be written as a similarity transformation. For a more detailed discussion on this topic, the reader may refer to
[
25
]
and
[
22
]
.

We now look at what linear combinations of
\Gamma_{k}
matrices yield another set of
2n
anti-commuting matrices. By explicitly writing the anti-commuting relations, we will obtain constraints on the linear combination coefficients of
\Gamma_{k}
’s.

Let us write
\Gamma_{k}^{*}
in terms of
\Gamma_{k}
,

\Gamma_{k}^{*}=\sum_{j=1}^{2n}o_{kj}\Gamma_{j}

(314)

We want this new set to be anti-commuting, that is, satisfy
(\Gamma_{k}^{*})^{2}=\mathds{1}
and
\Gamma_{k}^{*}\Gamma_{l}^{*}=\Gamma_{l}^{*}\Gamma_{k}^{*}
. We can explicitly write
\Gamma_{k}^{*}
matrices to see the constraints
o_{kj}
need to satisfy.

\displaystyle(\Gamma_{k}^{*})^{2}=

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{j=1}^{2n}o_{kj}\Gamma_{j}\right)\cdot\left(\sum_{l=1}^{2n}o_{kl}\Gamma_{l}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{2n}\sum_{l=1}^{2n}o_{kj}o_{kl}\Gamma_{j}\Gamma_{l}

We know that
\Gamma_{k}
anti-commute, thus for
j\neq l
we have
o_{kj}o_{kl}\Gamma_{j}\Gamma_{l}+o_{kl}o_{kj}\Gamma_{l}\Gamma_{j}=0
. We are now left with

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{2n}o_{kj}^{2}\mathds{1}\,.

Hence we see that
\sum_{j=1}^{2n}o_{kj}^{2}=1
should be satisfied.

We now look at the constraint on
o_{kj}
to satisfy the anti-commuting property. Suppose
k\neq l
.

\displaystyle\{\Gamma_{k}^{*},\Gamma_{l}^{*}\}=

\displaystyle\Gamma_{k}^{*}\Gamma_{l}^{*}+\Gamma_{l}^{*}\Gamma_{k}^{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{2n}\sum_{i=1}^{2n}o_{kj}o_{li}\Gamma_{j}\Gamma_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{2n}\sum_{j=1}^{2n}o_{li}o_{kj}\Gamma_{i}\Gamma_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{2n}\sum_{i=1}^{2n}o_{kj}o_{li}(\Gamma_{j}\Gamma_{i}+\Gamma_{i}\Gamma_{j})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{2n}o_{kj}o_{lj}\mathds{1}=0

Combining the two constraints we found on
o_{kj}
’s, we can say that in order to obtain a
2n
anti-commuting set from linear combinations of
\Gamma_{k}
, the coefficients
o_{kj}
must satisfy

\sum_{j=1}^{2n}o_{kj}o_{lj}=\delta_{kl}\,.

(315)

We can interpret
o_{kj}
as members of a
2n
-dimensional matrix. We can understand that the matrix is orthogonal from the properties of rotation matrices. We thus say that the rotation characterized by
o_{kj}
is
\bm{o}
which relates
\Gamma_{k}
and
\Gamma_{k}^{*}
,

\bm{o}:\Gamma_{k}\rightarrow\Gamma_{k}^{*}=\sum_{j=1}^{2n}o_{kj}\Gamma_{j}\,.

(316)

6.2.1
Spin representation of the orthogonal group

Since we know that there exists a transformation matrix
S
linking two sets, we write the similarity transformation matrix linking the sets of anti-commuting matrices obtained by applying
\bm{o}
as
S(\bm{o})
,

\Gamma_{k}^{*}=S(\bm{o})\Gamma_{k}S(\bm{o})^{-1}\,.

(317)

We now consider the full group of orthogonal matrices
\bm{o}
in
2n
-dim space. We will show that the collection of
S(\bm{o})
matrices forms a
2^{n}
-dimensional representation of this group. The first thing we need to show is that it is a group isomorphism, that is, for
\bm{o}\cdot\bm{o^{\prime}}=\bm{o^{\prime\prime}}
,

S(\bm{o})\cdot S(\bm{o^{\prime}})=S(\bm{o}\cdot\bm{o^{\prime}})=S(\bm{o^{\prime\prime}})\,.

(318)

We can show this by explicitly writing the transformations. We first write the transformations
\bm{o}
and
\bm{o^{\prime}}
.

\displaystyle\bm{o}:\Gamma_{k}\rightarrow\sum_{j=1}^{2n}o_{kj}\Gamma_{j}=S(\bm{o})\Gamma_{k}S(\bm{o})^{-1}

\displaystyle\bm{o^{\prime}}:\Gamma_{k}\rightarrow\sum_{i=1}^{2n}{o^{\prime}}_{ki}\Gamma_{i}=S(\bm{o^{\prime}})\Gamma_{k}S(\bm{o^{\prime}})^{-1}

Let us combine the transformations.

\displaystyle\bm{o^{\prime\prime}}=\bm{o}\cdot\bm{o^{\prime}}:\Gamma_{k}\rightarrow

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{2n}o_{kj}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{2n}{o^{\prime}}_{ji}\Gamma_{i}\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{2n}\sum_{i=1}^{2n}o_{kj}{o^{\prime}}_{ji}\Gamma_{i}

\displaystyle=S(\bm{o})S(\bm{o^{\prime}})\Gamma_{k}S(\bm{o^{\prime}})^{-1}S(\bm{o})^{-1}

By defining

{o^{\prime\prime}}_{ki}=\sum_{j=1}^{2n}o_{kj}{o^{\prime}}_{ji}

(319)

we can write

\displaystyle\bm{o^{\prime\prime}}:\Gamma_{k}\rightarrow

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{2n}{o^{\prime\prime}}_{ki}\Gamma_{i}

\displaystyle=S(\bm{o^{\prime\prime}})\Gamma_{k}S(\bm{o^{\prime\prime}})^{-1}\,.

Thus, we conclude that eq.
318
is satisfied.

We want to explicitly write what
S(\bm{o})
is. Notably, this is not a simple task on all occasions, but since we can realize the rotations of
2n

\Gamma
matrices as plane rotations, let’s see what we can do.

Consider the plane rotation K. It rotates
\Gamma_{k}
and
\Gamma_{l}
while fixing others.

\displaystyle\bm{K}:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{k}\rightarrow\cos{\theta}\cdot\Gamma_{k}-\sin{\theta}\cdot\Gamma_{l}\equiv\Gamma_{k}^{*}

\displaystyle\Gamma_{l}\rightarrow\sin{\theta}\cdot\Gamma_{k}+\cos{\theta}\cdot\Gamma_{l}\equiv\Gamma_{l}^{*}

\displaystyle\Gamma_{i}\rightarrow\Gamma_{i}\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{for $i\neq k,l$}

We will now show that

S(\bm{K})=\exp{\frac{\theta}{2}\cdot\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}}\,.

(320)

We can write the series expansion of
S(\bm{K})
.

\displaystyle\exp{\frac{\theta}{2}\cdot\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}}=

\displaystyle\mathds{1}+\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}+\frac{1}{2!}\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)^{2}(\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l})^{2}+\frac{1}{3!}\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)^{3}(\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l})^{3}+\frac{1}{4!}\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)^{4}(\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l})^{4}+\cdots

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\mathds{1}+\frac{1}{2!}\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)^{2}(\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l})^{2}+\frac{1}{4!}\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)^{4}(\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l})^{4}+\cdots\right)

\displaystyle+\left(\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}+\frac{1}{3!}\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)^{3}(\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l})^{3}+\frac{1}{5!}\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)^{5}(\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l})^{5}+\cdots\right)\,.

Let us look at the second half and explicitly write the terms; the logic is the same for the first half.

\displaystyle\left(\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}+\frac{1}{3!}\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)^{3}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}+\frac{1}{5!}\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)^{5}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}+\cdots\right)

We are working with anti-commuting matrices. Thus we know that
\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}=-\Gamma_{l}\Gamma_{k}
, so we can write

\displaystyle\left(\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}+(-1)\frac{1}{3!}\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)^{3}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}\Gamma_{l}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}+(-1)^{2}\frac{1}{5!}\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)^{5}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}\Gamma_{l}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}\Gamma_{l}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}+\cdots\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}-\frac{1}{3!}\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)^{3}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}+\frac{1}{5!}\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)^{5}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}+\cdots\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)-\frac{1}{3!}\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)^{3}+\frac{1}{5!}\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)^{5}+\cdots\right)\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}=\sin{\frac{\theta}{2}}\cdot\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}

The calculations in the first half are similar.

\displaystyle\left(\mathds{1}+\frac{1}{2!}\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)^{2}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}+\frac{1}{4!}\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)^{4}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}+\cdots\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathds{1}+(-1)\frac{1}{2!}\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)^{2}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}\Gamma_{l}+(-1)^{2}\frac{1}{4!}\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)^{4}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}\Gamma_{l}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}\Gamma_{l}+\cdots\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathds{1}+(-1)\frac{1}{2!}\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)^{2}\mathds{1}+(-1)^{2}\frac{1}{4!}\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)^{4}\mathds{1}+\cdots\right)=\cos{\frac{\theta}{2}}\cdot\mathds{1}

Thus,

\exp{\frac{\theta}{2}\cdot\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}}=\cos{\frac{\theta}{2}}\cdot\mathds{1}+\sin{\frac{\theta}{2}}\cdot\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}\,.

(321)

We can easily see that
S(\bm{K})^{-1}=\cos{\frac{\theta}{2}}\mathds{1}-\sin{\frac{\theta}{2}}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}
,

\displaystyle S(\bm{K})\cdot S(\bm{K})^{-1}

\displaystyle=(\cos{\frac{\theta}{2}}\mathds{1}+\sin{\frac{\theta}{2}}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l})\cdot(\cos{\frac{\theta}{2}}\mathds{1}-\sin{\frac{\theta}{2}}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l})

\displaystyle=\cos^{2}{\frac{\theta}{2}}\mathds{1}-\sin{\frac{\theta}{2}}\cos{\frac{\theta}{2}}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}+\cos{\frac{\theta}{2}}\sin{\frac{\theta}{2}}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l})-\sin^{2}{\frac{\theta}{2}}(\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l})^{2}

\displaystyle=\cos^{2}{\frac{\theta}{2}}\mathds{1}-(-1)\sin^{2}{\frac{\theta}{2}}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}\Gamma_{l}=\mathds{1}\,.

We can now explicitly write
\Gamma_{k}^{*}
,

\displaystyle S(\bm{K})\cdot\Gamma_{k}\cdot S(\bm{K})^{-1}

\displaystyle=(\cos{\frac{\theta}{2}}\mathds{1}+\sin{\frac{\theta}{2}}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l})\cdot\Gamma_{k}\cdot(\cos{\frac{\theta}{2}}\mathds{1}-\sin{\frac{\theta}{2}}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l})

\displaystyle=\left(\cos{\frac{\theta}{2}}\Gamma_{k}-\sin{\frac{\theta}{2}\Gamma_{l}}\right)\cdot\left(\cos{\frac{\theta}{2}}\mathds{1}-\sin{\frac{\theta}{2}}\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}\right)

\displaystyle=\cos^{2}{\frac{\theta}{2}}\Gamma_{k}-\cos{\frac{\theta}{2}}\sin{\frac{\theta}{2}}\Gamma_{l}-\sin{\frac{\theta}{2}}\cos{\frac{\theta}{2}}\Gamma_{l}-\sin^{2}{\frac{\theta}{2}}\Gamma_{k}

\displaystyle=\left(\cos^{2}{\frac{\theta}{2}}-\sin^{2}{\frac{\theta}{2}}\right)\Gamma_{k}-\left(2\cos{\frac{\theta}{2}}\sin{\frac{\theta}{2}}\right)\Gamma_{l}

\displaystyle=\cos{\theta}\cdot\Gamma_{k}-\sin{\theta}\cdot\Gamma_{l}=\Gamma_{k}^{*}\,.

Similarly,
S(\bm{K})\cdot\Gamma_{l}\cdot S(\bm{K})^{-1}=\sin{\theta}\cdot\Gamma_{k}+\cos{\theta}\cdot\Gamma_{l}=\Gamma_{l}^{*}

The half angle seen in eq.
320
provides a double-valued representation, which is a characteristic feature of spin representations. To understand it more deeply, let us do a little experiment. Let
\bm{K^{\prime}}
be the rotation characterized by
\theta+2\pi
. Since adding
2\pi
does not affect the rotation, this might seen like a redundant thing to do, however, if we look at
S(\bm{K^{\prime}})
we see that it does get affected.

\displaystyle S(\bm{K^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\cos{\frac{\theta+2\pi}{2}}\cdot\mathds{1}+\sin{\frac{\theta+2\pi}{2}}\cdot\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}

\displaystyle=-\cos{\frac{\theta}{2}}\cdot\mathds{1}-\sin{\frac{\theta}{2}}\cdot\Gamma_{k}\Gamma_{l}=-S(\bm{K})

Thus, it can be seen that the number of full turns done before the rotation changes the value.

6.2.2
Eigenvalues of the spin representatives

We want to explicitly write what
S(\bm{o})
is. It is worth noting that this is not a simple task on all occasions, but we can use it since we are working with plane rotations.

Consider the plane rotation K, as defined in the previous sections. We can write
S(\bm{K})
easily if it acts on
\Gamma^{*}_{2r-1}
and
\Gamma^{*}_{2r}
.

\displaystyle S(\bm{K})

\displaystyle=\exp{\frac{\theta}{2}\cdot\Gamma^{*}_{2r-1}\Gamma^{*}_{2r}}=\exp{\frac{\theta}{2}\cdot iZ_{r}}

\displaystyle=\cos{\frac{\theta}{2}}\cdot\mathds{1}+i\sin{\frac{\theta}{2}}\cdot Z_{r}

\displaystyle=(\mathds{1}_{1}\cdot\mathds{1}_{2}\cdots\begin{pmatrix}\cos{\frac{\theta}{2}}&\\
&\cos{\frac{\theta}{2}}\end{pmatrix})\cdots\mathds{1}_{2n})+(\mathds{1}_{1}\cdot\mathds{1}_{2}\cdots\begin{pmatrix}i\sin{\frac{\theta}{2}}&\\
&-i\sin{\frac{\theta}{2}}\end{pmatrix})\cdots\mathds{1}_{n})

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}_{1}\cdot\mathds{1}_{2}\cdots\begin{pmatrix}e^{i\theta/2}&\\
&e^{-i\theta/2}\end{pmatrix}\cdots\mathds{1}_{n}

Thus we see that the eigenvalues of
S(\bm{K})
are of the form
e^{i\theta/2}
and
e^{-i\theta/2}
with each having
2^{n-1}
degeneracy. This is a consequence of the
n-1

\mathds{1}
matrices in the direct product. Remark that
\bm{K}
has eigenvalues
e^{i\theta/2}
,
e^{-i\theta/2}
and
1
, where
1
has
2(n-1)
degeneracy because of the
2n-2

\Gamma
matrices it fixes.

We now consider a product of
n
commuting matrices. To obtain
n
commuting matrices from rotations acting on
2n

\Gamma
matrices, we pair
\Gamma
matrices and consider the rotations acting on them. Because no
\Gamma_{i}
appears on two rotations, we can clearly see that they commute. We can write this product of rotations as

\bm{K}=\prod_{r=1}^{n}\bm{K}_{r}\,.

(322)

The eigenvalues of
\bm{K}
are

e^{\pm i\theta_{1}/2},\,e^{\pm i\theta_{2}/2},\cdots,e^{\pm i\theta_{n}/2}\,.

(323)

We can explicitly write
S(\bm{K})
since we now the representations of
S(\bm{K_{i}})
,

S(\bm{K})=\prod_{r=1}^{n}S(\bm{K_{r}})=\prod_{r=1}^{n}\exp{\frac{\theta_{r}}{2}\cdot\Gamma_{r_{1}}\Gamma_{r_{2}}}\,.

(324)

If we consider the case
\Gamma_{r_{1}}=\Gamma_{2r-1}
and
\Gamma_{r_{2}}=\Gamma_{2r}
, then we can write

\displaystyle S(\bm{K})

\displaystyle=\prod_{r=1}^{n}\exp{\frac{\theta_{r}}{2}\cdot\Gamma_{2r-1}\Gamma_{2r}}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}e^{i\theta_{1}/2}&\\
&e^{-i\theta_{1}/2}\end{pmatrix}\times\begin{pmatrix}e^{i\theta_{2}/2}&\\
&e^{-i\theta_{2}/2}\end{pmatrix}\times\cdots\begin{pmatrix}e^{i\theta_{n}/2}&\\
&e^{-i\theta_{n}/2}\end{pmatrix}

(325)

With
2^{n}
eigenvalues

\displaystyle\lambda=\exp{\frac{i}{2}\cdot\left(\pm\theta_{1}\pm\theta_{2}\pm\cdots\pm\theta_{n}\right)}

where every combination of
\pm
is taken.

Remember that we had said there exists a basis transformation between any given
2n
anti-commuting
\Gamma
matrices. Since we know that eigenvalues are invariant under basis transformations, we can conclude that for any rotation
\bm{o}
given with eigenvalues

\displaystyle e^{-i\theta_{1}},\,e^{-i\theta_{2}},\cdots,e^{-i\theta_{n}}

(326)

the spin representative
S(\bm{o})
has the eigenvalues

\displaystyle\exp{\frac{i}{2}\cdot\left(\pm\theta_{1}\pm\theta_{2}\pm\cdots\pm\theta_{n}\right)}\,.

6.3
Spinor analysis of the transfer matrix

We now have the necessary tools to find the eigenvalues of the
V
matrix.

We now write another set of
\Gamma_{r}^{*}
matrices by performing a similarity transformation
g
which changes the places of
X
and
Z
in
\Gamma_{r}
. We can write the
g
matrix explicitly as

g=2^{\frac{-n}{2}}\left((X_{1}+Z_{1})\cdot(X_{2}+Z_{2})\cdots(X_{n}+Z_{n})\right)\,.

(327)

We can see that
g=g^{-1}
easily, since

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(X+Z)(X+Z)=\frac{1}{2}X^{2}+XZ+ZX+Z^{2}=\frac{1}{2}2\mathds{1}+iY-iY=\mathds{1}\,.

Now, let’s look at how
g
would act on spin matrices.

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left((X+Z)X(X+Z)\right)=\frac{1}{2}\left(X+ZX^{2}+Z+ZXZ\right)=Z

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left((X+Z)Y(X+Z)\right)=\frac{1}{2}\left(iZX-iX^{2}+iZ^{2}-iXZ\right)=-Y

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left((X+Z)Z(X+Z)\right)=\frac{1}{2}\left(XZX+X+XZ^{2}+Z\right)=X

By applying
g
on
\Gamma_{r}
matrices, we can write

\displaystyle g\Gamma_{2r-1}g^{-1}=\Gamma_{2r-1}^{*}=Z_{1}\cdots Z_{r-1}\cdot X_{r}\cdot\mathds{1}_{r+1}\cdot\mathds{1}_{n}\equiv P_{r}^{*}

\displaystyle g\Gamma_{2r}g^{-1}=\Gamma_{2r}^{*}=Z_{1}\cdots Z_{r-1}\cdot Y_{r}\cdot\mathds{1}_{r+1}\cdot\mathds{1}_{n}\equiv Q_{r}^{*}

(328)

which is a perfectly good realization of a set of
2n
anti-commuting matrices.

We can express our
V_{a}
and
V_{b}
matrices in terms of
\Gamma_{r}^{*}
matrices.

Let us look at the structure of these matrices. We can explicitly write the matrix
i\Gamma_{2r-1}^{*}\Gamma_{2r}^{*}
,

\displaystyle i\Gamma_{2r-1}^{*}\Gamma_{2r}^{*}

\displaystyle=i(Z_{1}Z_{2}\cdots X_{r}\cdot\mathds{1}_{r+1}\cdots\mathds{1}_{n})\cdot(Z_{1}Z_{2}\cdots Y_{r}\cdot\mathds{1}_{r+1}\cdots\mathds{1}_{n})

\displaystyle=(\mathds{1}_{1}\cdots\mathds{1}_{r-1}\cdot(-Z_{r})\cdot\mathds{1}_{r+1}\cdots\mathds{1}_{n})\,,

which is seen to have a diagonal form. Whereas, the matrix
-i\Gamma_{2r+1}^{*}\Gamma_{2r}^{*}
does not have a diagonal form.

\displaystyle-i\Gamma_{2r+1}^{*}\Gamma_{2r}^{*}

\displaystyle=-i(Z_{1}Z_{2}\cdots\cdots Z_{r}X_{r+1}\cdot\mathds{1}_{r+1}\cdots\mathds{1}_{n})\cdot(Z_{1}Z_{2}\cdots Y\cdot\mathds{1}_{r+1}\cdots\mathds{1}_{n})

\displaystyle=(\mathds{1}_{1}\cdots\mathds{1}_{r-1}\cdot(-X_{r})\cdot(x_{r+1})\cdot\mathds{1}_{r+1}\cdots\mathds{1}_{n})\,.

Before moving further, it is worth pointing out that we can write a transformation between
V_{a}
and
V_{b}
through the rotation
D
that acts as

\displaystyle D:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{2r-1}\rightarrow\Gamma_{2r+1}

(329)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{2r}\rightarrow\Gamma_{2r}\,.

(330)

If we write the matrices
V_{a}
and
V_{b}
depending on the interactions between rows and columns respectively,
that is
V_{a}(a)
and
V_{b}(b)
, then

\displaystyle D:

\displaystyle V_{a}(a)\rightarrow V_{b}(a^{\prime})

(331)

\displaystyle V_{b}(b)\rightarrow V_{a}(b^{\prime})\,.

(332)

Thus for a lattice
V(a,b)
, there corresponds a dual lattice
V(b^{\prime},a^{\prime})
which has the same partition function except for the factor of
(\sinh{2a}\cdot\sinh{2b})^{-mn/2}
caused by the boundary conditions.

6.4
The eigenvalues and eigenvectors of
V

We will now use the fact that
V_{a}
and
V_{b}
are spin representations of certain rotations, say,
R_{a}
and
R_{b}
. Then clearly,
V
is the spin representative of
R_{a}\cdot R_{b}
. Thus if we can find the eigenvalues of
R_{a}\cdot R_{b}
, then we easily obtain the eigenvalues of
V
. The similarity between
V_{a}
and
V_{b}
mentioned in the previous section necessitates a relation between
R_{a}
and
R_{b}
, which we will see that one can be obtained from the other by a permutation of rows and columns.

We now express
V_{a}
and
V_{b}
in the form of eq.
324
.

\displaystyle V_{b}=\prod_{r=1}^{n}\exp{ib^{\prime}\Gamma_{2r-1}\Gamma_{2r}}

\displaystyle V_{a}=\prod_{r=1}^{n-1}\exp{-ia\Gamma_{2r+1}\Gamma_{2r}}\cdot\exp{ia\Gamma_{1}\Gamma_{2n}}

(333)

In this notation of
V_{b}
and
V_{a}
),
\frac{1}{2}\theta_{b_{r}}=ib^{\prime}
and
\frac{1}{2}\theta_{a_{r}}=ia
) for
r=1,\cdots,n
. Hence we can say that all of the factors of
V_{b}
and
V_{a}
except
\exp{ia\Gamma_{1}\Gamma_{2n}}
are representatives of plane rotations. The last factor of
V_{a}
indicates a boundary condition on the system. Since we are working on a 2-dimensional lattice model, putting boundary conditions on one of the sides gives a cylinder.

We know define an involution
U
, to factor in the boundary factor
\exp{ia\Gamma_{1}\Gamma_{2n}}
. We define projection operators
P^{+}=\frac{1}{2}(1+U)
and
P^{-}=\frac{1}{2}(1-U)
. We then define

V^{\pm}=P^{\pm}V\,.

(334)

Now, we can change our original coordinate by the transformation matrix
g
that interchanges
X
and
Z
matrices, which is given in eq.
327
. Let us now look at what happens to
B
and
A
matrices given in eq.
304
under this coordinate change.

\displaystyle g\left(\sum_{r=1}^{n}X_{r}\right)g=\sum_{r=1}^{n}Z_{r}\equiv B^{\prime}

(335)

\displaystyle g\left(\sum_{r=1}^{n}Z_{r}Z_{r+1}\right)g=\sum_{r=1}^{n}X_{r}X_{r+1}\equiv A^{\prime}

(336)

Our involution
U
has the form now has the form

U^{\prime}=Z_{1}\cdot Z_{2}\cdots Z_{n}

(337)

in this basis.

Since
U^{\prime}
is a diagonal matrix with half of its entries
1
and the other half
-1
, we can reorganize the rows and columns to obtain a matrix of the form
\begin{pmatrix}\mathds{1}&\\
&-\mathds{1}\end{pmatrix}
.

We remark that
U^{\prime}
commutes with
A^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime}
.

\displaystyle U^{\prime}\cdot A^{\prime}=\sum_{r=1}^{n}(Z_{1}\cdot Z_{2}\cdots Z_{n})\cdot X_{r}X_{r+1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{n}X_{r}X_{r+1}\cdot(Z_{1}\cdot Z_{2}\cdots Z_{n})=A^{\prime}\cdot U^{\prime}

since

\displaystyle(Z_{1}\cdot Z_{2}\cdots Z_{n})\cdot X_{r}X_{r+1}=(-i)^{2}Z_{1}\cdots Y_{r}Y_{r+1}\cdots Z_{n}

\displaystyle=(i)^{2}Z_{1}\cdots Y_{r}Y_{r+1}\cdots Z_{n}=X_{r}X_{r+1}\cdot(Z_{1}\cdot Z_{2}\cdots Z_{n})

Similarly,

\displaystyle U^{\prime}\cdot B^{\prime}=\sum_{r=1}^{n}(Z_{1}\cdot Z_{2}\cdots Z_{n})\cdot Z_{r}=\sum_{r=1}^{n}Z_{r}\cdot(Z_{1}\cdot Z_{2}\cdots Z_{n})=B^{\prime}=U^{\prime}

Thus,
[U^{\prime},B^{\prime}]=0
and
[U^{\prime},A^{\prime}]=0
. In general, if a matrix
M
commutes with
U^{\prime}
, then it has the form

\displaystyle M=\begin{pmatrix}M_{1}&0\\
0&M_{2}\end{pmatrix}\,,

and if a matrix
N
anti-commutes with
U^{\prime}
, then it has the form

\displaystyle N=\begin{pmatrix}0&N_{1}\\
N_{2}&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

From this, we can conclude that the form
V^{\prime}
is

\displaystyle V^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}V^{\prime}_{1}&0\\
0&V^{\prime}_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathds{1}&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}V^{{}^{\prime}+}_{1}&0\\
0&V^{{}^{\prime}+}_{2}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&\mathds{1}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}V^{{}^{\prime}-}_{1}&0\\
0&V^{{}^{\prime}-}_{2}\end{pmatrix}\,.

We now have separated the upper and lower halves of the matrix
V^{\prime}
. Thus, we can diagonalize them separately and then choose the relevant half of the eigenvalues of
V^{{}^{\prime}+}
and
V^{{}^{\prime}-}
matrices.

Separating
V^{\prime}
into two parts gives us the freedom to diagonalize each part separately. It will be later seen that the transformations involved in diagonalizing will be representations of orthogonal rotations, say
T^{+}
and
T^{-}
, which will act on
U^{\prime}
as

\displaystyle T^{+}:U^{\prime}\rightarrow U^{\prime}

\displaystyle T^{-}:U^{\prime}\rightarrow-U^{\prime}\,.

(338)

Thus, we can see that

\displaystyle S(T^{+})\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2}(1+U^{\prime})\cdot V^{{}^{\prime}+}\right)S(T^{+})^{-1}

(339)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(1+U^{\prime})\cdot S(T^{+})\cdot V^{{}^{\prime}+}\cdot S(T^{+})^{-1}

(340)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(1+U^{\prime})\cdot S(T^{+}\cdot R^{+}\cdot(T^{+})^{-1})

(341)

and

\displaystyle S(T^{-})\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2}(1-U^{\prime})\cdot V^{{}^{\prime}-}\right)S(T^{-})^{-1}

(342)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(1+U^{\prime})\cdot S(T^{-})\cdot V^{{}^{\prime}-}\cdot S(T^{-})^{-1}

(343)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(1+U^{\prime})\cdot S(T^{-}\cdot R^{-}\cdot(T^{-})^{-1})

(344)

Now, suppose that we have already found the
2n
-eigenvalues of
R^{-}
,
\exp{\pm\gamma_{2r}}
. Then, we know that the diagonal matrix

S(T^{-})\cdot V^{-}\cdot S(T^{-})^{-1}=\prod_{r=1}^{n}\exp{\frac{i}{2}\gamma_{2r}\Gamma_{2r-1}^{*}\Gamma_{2r}^{*}}\equiv\Lambda^{-}

(345)

which has the eigenvalues

\exp{\frac{1}{2}(\pm\gamma_{2}\pm\gamma_{4}\pm\cdots\pm\gamma_{2n})}\,.

(346)

The sign of each component depends on the value of the components of
\Gamma_{2r-1}^{*}\Gamma_{2r}^{*}
. We know that the factor
\frac{1}{2}(1+U^{\prime})
keeps only half of the eigenvalues that fall in the upper half. Thus

U^{\prime}_{ii}=1=\prod_{r=1}^{n}(\Gamma_{2r-1}^{*}\Gamma_{2r}^{*})_{ii}\,.

Yet,
(\Gamma_{2r-1}^{*}\Gamma_{2r}^{*})_{ii}=\pm 1
, and for
U^{\prime}_{ii}=1
, only an even number of
(\Gamma_{2r-1}^{*}\Gamma_{2r}^{*})_{ii}
can be negative. Hence we can conclude that the eigenvalues that we should keep are the ones with an even number of negative
\gamma_{2r}
’s.

The case is similar for
V^{{}^{\prime}+}
. Let the eigenvalues of
R^{+}
be
\exp{\pm\gamma_{2r-1}}
, then the eigenvalues of
V^{+}
are of the form

\exp{\frac{1}{2}(\pm\gamma_{1}\pm\gamma_{3}\pm\cdots\pm\gamma_{2n-1})}\,.

(347)

Again, by taking the factor
\frac{1}{2}(1+U^{\prime})
we can say that the only the eigenvalues with an even number of
\gamma_{2r-1}
will survive.

We remark that the angles
\gamma_{2r}
and
\gamma_{2r-1}
are the angles of rotations of
R^{-}
and
R^{+}
respectively, which are found by diagonalizing the respective
2n
-dimensional matrices.

6.5
The Complete Partition Function

We can now write the complete partition function

Z=\frac{1}{2}(2\sinh{2b})^{nm/2}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{2^{n}}\lambda_{i}^{m}

(348)

as

Z=\frac{1}{2}(2\sinh{2b})^{nm/2}\cdot\left(\sum\exp{\frac{m}{2}(\pm\gamma_{2}\pm\cdots\pm\gamma_{2n})}+\sum\exp{\frac{m}{2}(\pm\gamma_{1}\pm\cdots\pm\gamma_{2n-1})}\right)\,.

(349)

To write in a more compact form, we can write

\displaystyle Z=\frac{1}{2}(2\sinh{2b})^{\frac{nm}{2}}\cdot\left(\prod_{r=1}^{n}(2\cosh{\frac{m}{2}\gamma_{2r}})+\prod_{r=1}^{n}(2\sinh{\frac{m}{2}\gamma_{2r}})+\prod_{r=1}^{n}(2\cosh{\frac{m}{2}\gamma_{2r-1}})+\prod_{r=1}^{n}(2\sinh{\frac{m}{2}\gamma_{2r-1}})\right)

(350)

6.5.1
Explicitly calculating the partition function

We can now move to explicitly calculating the eigenvalues of
V^{+}
and
V^{-}
. Since working with a matrix in a symmetric form

V_{b}^{1/2}V_{a}V_{b}^{1/2}\equiv V_{0}

is easier, we will first find the eigenvectors of
V_{0}
and then obtain the eigenvectors of
V
by applying a
V_{b}^{1/2}=\exp{\frac{1}{2}bB}
.
We have

V_{0}^{-}=\prod_{r=1}^{n}\exp{\frac{i}{2}b\Gamma_{2r-1}\Gamma_{2r}}\cdot\prod_{r=1}^{n}\exp{-ia\Gamma_{2r-1}\Gamma_{2r}}\cdot\prod_{r=1}^{n}\exp{\frac{i}{2}b\Gamma_{2r-1}\Gamma_{2r}}

where

\prod_{r=1}^{n}\exp{\frac{i}{2}b\Gamma_{2r-1}\Gamma_{2r}}=\begin{pmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}R_{b}\end{bmatrix}&0&\cdots&0\\
0&\begin{bmatrix}R_{b}\end{bmatrix}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&\begin{bmatrix}R_{b}\end{bmatrix}\end{pmatrix}\,

and

\prod_{r=1}^{n}\exp{-ia\Gamma_{2r-1}\Gamma_{2r}}=\begin{pmatrix}\cosh{2a}&0&\cdots&0&\pm i\sinh{2a}\\
0&\begin{bmatrix}R_{2a}\end{bmatrix}&\cdots&0&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&\begin{bmatrix}R_{2a}\end{bmatrix}&0\\
\mp i\sinh{2a}&0&\cdots&0&\cosh{2a}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(351)

where

\begin{bmatrix}R_{i}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\cosh{i}&i\sinh{i}\\
-i\sinh{i}&\cosh{i}\end{bmatrix}\,.

(352)

Now we can write
V_{0}^{-}
,

V_{0}^{\pm}=\begin{pmatrix}A&B&0&\cdots&0&\mp B^{\dagger}\\
B^{\dagger}&A&B&\cdots&0&0\\
0&B^{\dagger}&A&\cdots&0&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\cdots&A&B\\
\mp B&0&0&\cdots&B^{\dagger}&A\end{pmatrix}\,,

(353)

where

\displaystyle A=\cosh{2a}\begin{pmatrix}\cosh{2\bar{b}}&i\sinh{2\bar{b}}\\
-i\sinh{2\bar{b}}&\cosh{2\bar{b}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle B=\sinh{2a}\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{1}{2}\sinh{2\bar{b}}&-i\sinh^{2}{\bar{b}}\\
i\cosh^{2}{\bar{b}}&-\frac{1}{2}\sinh{2\bar{b}}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(354)

By using the properties of cyclic matrices, we can deduce the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of this matrix. The form of the eigenvectors and eigenvalues of circulant matrices are well known. Using this ansatz, we can say that

\phi=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\begin{pmatrix}zu\\
z^{2}u\\
\vdots\\
z^{n}u\end{pmatrix}\,,

(355)

where
z\in\mathbb{C}
and
u
is a two-component vector such that
u^{\dagger}u=1
.
From the eigenvector condition
\mathcal{V}^{\pm}\phi=\lambda\phi
we get
n
equations

\displaystyle(zA+z^{2}B\mp z^{n}B^{\dagger})u

\displaystyle=z\lambda u

(356)

\displaystyle(z^{2}A+z^{3}B+zB^{\dagger})u

\displaystyle=z^{2}\lambda u

(357)

\displaystyle(z^{3}A+z^{4}B+z^{2}B^{\dagger})u

\displaystyle=z^{3}\lambda u

(358)

(359)

\displaystyle(z^{n-1}A+z^{n}B+z^{n-2}B^{\dagger})u

\displaystyle=z^{n-1}\lambda u

(360)

\displaystyle(z^{n}A\mp zB+z^{n-1}B^{\dagger})u

\displaystyle=z^{n}\lambda u\,.

(361)

The equations apart from the first and last ones are identical. Therefore these
n
equations can be reduced to three independent equations:

\displaystyle(A+zB\mp z^{n-1}B^{\dagger})u

\displaystyle=\lambda u

(362)

\displaystyle(A+zB+z^{-1}B^{\dagger})u

\displaystyle=\lambda u

(363)

\displaystyle(A\mp z^{1-n}B+z^{-1}B^{\dagger})u

\displaystyle=\lambda u\,.

(364)

If we set
z^{n}=\mp 1
, then these three equations become identical,

(A+zB+z^{-1}B^{\dagger})u=\lambda u\,.

(365)

We can see that if
z_{k}=e^{i\pi k/n}
, for
k=\bar{0,1,\cdots,2n-1}
, then there are a total of
2n
values that solve
z=\mp 1
, where odd
k
lead to
-1
and even
k
lead to
1
. For each
k
, we now need to find the associated eigenvalues
\lambda^{\uparrow\downarrow}_{k}
and eigenvectors
u^{\uparrow\downarrow}_{k}
. Now, let us define a matrix

M_{k}=A+e^{i\pi k/n}B+e^{-i\pi k/n}B^{\dagger}=\begin{pmatrix}d_{k}&o_{k}\\
o^{*}_{k}&d_{k}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(366)

Here,

d_{k}=\cosh{2a}\cosh{2\bar{b}}-\cos{w_{k}}\sinh{2\bar{b}}\sinh{2a}

(367)

and

o_{k}=-\sin{w_{k}}\sinh{2\bar{b}}+i(\sinh{2a}\cosh{2\bar{b}}-\cos{\frac{\pi k}{n}}\cosh{2a}\sinh{2\bar{b}})\,,

(368)

where
w_{k}=\frac{\pi k}{n}
.

We can explicitly calculate the determinant of
M_{k}
matrix

\displaystyle\det M_{k}=

\displaystyle(\cosh{2a}\cosh{2\bar{b}}-\cos{\frac{\pi k}{n}}\sinh{2a}\sinh{2\bar{b}})^{2}

\displaystyle-(-\sin{\frac{\pi k}{n}}\sinh{2\bar{b}}+i(\sinh{2a}\cosh{2\bar{b}}-\cos{\frac{\pi k}{n}}\cosh{2a}\sinh{2\bar{b}}))

\displaystyle\cdot(-\sin{\frac{\pi k}{n}}\sinh{2\bar{b}}-i(\sinh{2a}\cosh{2\bar{b}}-\cos{\frac{\pi k}{n}}\cosh{2a}\sinh{2\bar{b}}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\cosh{2a}\cosh{2\bar{b}})^{2}-(\sinh{2a}\cosh{2\bar{b}})^{2}+(\cos{\frac{\pi k}{n}})^{2}(\sinh{2a}\sinh{2\bar{b}})^{2}

\displaystyle-(\sin{\frac{\pi k}{n}})^{2}(\sinh{2\bar{b}})^{2}+2(\sinh{a}\cosh{2\bar{b}}\cos{\frac{\pi k}{n}}\cosh{2a}\sinh{2\bar{b}}

\displaystyle-\cosh{2a}\cosh{2\bar{b}}\cos{\frac{\pi k}{n}}\sinh{a}\sinh{2\bar{b}})-(\cos{\frac{\pi k}{n}})^{2}(\cosh{2a}\sinh{2\bar{b}})^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\cosh^{2}{2\bar{b}}(\cosh^{2}{2a}-\sinh^{2}{2a})+(\cos{\frac{\pi k}{n}})^{2}(\sinh^{2}{2\bar{b}}(\sinh^{2}{a}-\cosh^{2}{a}))-(\sin{\frac{\pi k}{n}})^{2}(\sinh^{2}{2\bar{b}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\cosh^{2}{2\bar{b}}-\sinh^{2}{2\bar{b}}((\cos{\frac{\pi k}{n}})^{2}+(\sin{\frac{\pi k}{n}})^{2})=1

The unit determinant implies eigenvalues of the form

\displaystyle\lambda^{\uparrow}_{k}=e^{+\gamma}

\displaystyle\lambda^{\downarrow}_{k}=e^{-\gamma}

(369)

with
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
. We can use the trace to calculate the value of
\gamma
.

tr(M_{k})=2(\cosh{2a}\cosh{2\bar{b}}-\cos{\frac{\pi k}{n}}\sinh{2a}\sinh{2\bar{b}})=e^{+\gamma}+e^{-\gamma}=2\cosh{\gamma}

(370)

It can be clearly seen that this equation is the law of cosine for the hyperbolic triangles. From a geometrical perspective,
\gamma_{2r}
is the third side of the hyperbolic triangle whose other sides are
2a
and
2\bar{b}
, which includes the angle
w_{k}=\frac{k\pi}{n}
, this hyperbolic triangle is illustrated in Fig.
56
.

w_{k}
2\bar{b}
2a
\gamma_{k}
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
\bar{\delta}
\delta

Figure 56
:
The hyperbolic triangle.

We can use the properties of hyperbolic triangles to simplify the
M_{k}
matrix. These properties are explained in
[
17
]
. The equations we will use are

\displaystyle\cosh{\gamma_{k}}=\cosh{2a}\cdot\cosh{2\bar{b}}-\sinh{2a}\cdot\sinh{2\bar{b}}\cdot\cos{w_{k}}

(371)

\displaystyle\sinh{\gamma_{k}}\cdot\cos{\delta^{\prime}}=\sinh{2\bar{b}}\cdot\cosh{2}-\cosh{2\bar{b}}\cdot\sinh{2a}\cdot\cos{w_{k}}

(372)

\displaystyle\frac{\sin{w_{k}}}{\sinh{\gamma_{k}}}=\frac{\sin{\delta^{\prime}}}{\sinh{2a}}=\frac{\sin{\delta^{*}}}{\sinh{2\bar{b}}}

(373)

Using these equations, we can write

\displaystyle d_{k}

\displaystyle=\cosh{2a}\cdot\cosh{2\bar{b}}-\cos{w_{k}}\cdot\sinh{2a}\cdot\sinh{2\bar{b}}=\cosh{\gamma_{k}}

\displaystyle o_{k}

\displaystyle=-\sin{w_{k}}\sinh{2\bar{b}}+i(\sinh{2a}\cosh{2\bar{b}}-\cos{\frac{\pi k}{n}}\cosh{2a}\sinh{2\bar{b}})

\displaystyle=\sin{\delta^{\prime}}\cdot\sinh{\gamma_{k}}-i\cosh{2a}\cdot\sinh{2\bar{b}}+i\cos{w_{k}}\cdot\sinh{2a}\cdot\cosh{2\bar{b}}

\displaystyle=\sinh{\gamma_{k}}\cdot(\sin{\delta}-i\cos{\delta}).

Thus, we can write

\displaystyle M_{k}=\begin{pmatrix}\cosh{\gamma_{k}}&\sinh{\gamma_{k}}(\sin{\delta_{k}}-i\cos{\delta_{k}})\\
\sinh{\gamma_{k}}(\sin{\delta_{k}}+i\cos{\delta_{k}})&\cosh{\gamma_{k}}\end{pmatrix}=\cosh{\gamma_{k}}\mathds{1}+i\sinh{\gamma_{k}}\begin{pmatrix}0&-e^{i\delta_{k}}\\
e^{-i\delta_{k}}&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

Now
M_{k}
’s eigenvectors
u_{k}
’s can be easily found. We see that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}e^{i\frac{\delta_{k}}{2}}\\
ie^{-i\frac{\delta_{k}}{2}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}ie^{i\frac{\delta_{k}}{2}}\\
e^{-i\frac{\delta_{k}}{2}}\end{pmatrix}\,.

Thus, we can now write the eigenvectors (
355
) of
V_{0}^{\pm}
explicitly,

\displaystyle\phi_{k}^{\uparrow}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\begin{pmatrix}e^{i\left(w_{k}+\frac{\delta_{k}}{2}\right)}\\
ie^{i\left(w_{k}-\frac{\delta_{k}}{2}\right)}\\
e^{i\left(2w_{k}+\frac{\delta_{k}}{2}\right)}\\
ie^{i\left(2w_{k}-\frac{\delta_{k}}{2}\right)}\\
\vdots\\
e^{i\left(nw_{k}+\frac{\delta_{k}}{2}\right)}\\
ie^{i\left(nw_{k}-\frac{\delta_{k}}{2}\right)}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\phi_{k}^{\downarrow}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\begin{pmatrix}ie^{i\left(w_{k}+\frac{\delta_{k}}{2}\right)}\\
e^{i\left(w_{k}-\frac{\delta_{k}}{2}\right)}\\
ie^{i\left(2w_{k}+\frac{\delta_{k}}{2}\right)}\\
e^{i\left(2w_{k}-\frac{\delta_{k}}{2}\right)}\\
\vdots\\
ie^{i\left(nw_{k}+\frac{\delta_{k}}{2}\right)}\\
e^{i\left(nw_{k}-\frac{\delta_{k}}{2}\right)}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(374)

To diagonalize
R^{\pm}
, we write
E^{\pm}
matrices such that

\displaystyle E_{+}^{-1}=(\phi^{\uparrow}_{1},\phi^{\downarrow}_{2n-1},\phi^{\uparrow}_{3},\phi^{\downarrow}_{2n-3},\cdots,\phi^{\uparrow}_{2n-3}\phi^{\downarrow}_{3},\phi^{\uparrow}_{2n-1},\phi^{\downarrow}_{1})

(375)

\displaystyle E_{-}^{-1}=(\phi^{\uparrow}_{0},\phi^{\downarrow}_{0},\phi^{\uparrow}_{2},\phi^{\downarrow}_{2n-2},\cdots,\phi^{\uparrow}_{2n-4}\phi^{\downarrow}_{4},\phi^{\uparrow}_{2n-2},\phi^{\downarrow}_{2})\,.

(376)

Our aim is to represent these matrices in the spin space. Yet neither
E^{\pm}
nor
\Lambda^{\pm}
is in orthogonal form. Thus we need a further transformation
T_{0}
so that

T^{\pm}_{0}E^{\pm}R^{\pm}(E^{\pm})^{-1}{T^{\pm}_{0}}^{-1}=K^{\pm}

where
K^{\pm}
is a rotation. Let us define
T^{\pm}=T_{0}E^{\pm}
. We choose
T^{\pm}_{0}
such that it brings
\Lambda
to its canonical form and simultaneously makes
T^{\pm}
orthogonal.

Since we have shown that
V^{\pm}
can be written as spin representatives, we are certain that such
T
can be written, we only to need to verify that
T
is also in the spin space, thus we check if
T^{\pm}
is orthogonal.
The transformation
T^{-}
acts as

\displaystyle T^{-}:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{2a-1}\rightarrow\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\left(\sum_{r=1}^{n}\cos{\left(w_{a}\cdot 2r+\frac{\delta_{2r}^{\prime}}{2}\right)}\Gamma_{2r-1}-\sum_{r=1}^{n}\sin{\left(w_{a}\cdot 2r+\frac{\delta_{2r}^{\prime}}{2}\right)}\Gamma_{2r}\right)

(377)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{2a}\rightarrow\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\left(\sum_{r=1}^{n}\sin{\left(w_{a}\cdot 2r-\frac{\delta_{2r}^{\prime}}{2}\right)}\Gamma_{2r-1}-\sum_{r=1}^{n}\cos{\left(w_{a}\cdot 2r-\frac{\delta_{2r}^{\prime}}{2}\right)}\Gamma_{2r}\right)\,.

(378)

We can see that the determinant of
T^{-}
is
-1
, an improper rotation; thus, T is orthogonal. Therefore,

T^{\pm}\cdot R^{\pm}\cdot(T^{\pm})^{-1}=K^{\pm}\,,

and in the spin space,

S(T^{-})\cdot V_{0}^{-}\cdot S((T^{-}))^{-1}=S(K^{-})=\prod_{r=1}^{n}\exp{i(\frac{\gamma_{r}}{2})\Gamma_{2r-1}\Gamma_{2r}}

(379)

Remark that because of our basis choice,
S(K^{\pm})
is not diagonal since
i\Gamma_{2r-1}\Gamma_{2r}=X_{r}
. Thus, we need to apply one more transformation to
S(K^{\pm})
in order to obtain a diagonal spin representative, the basis change transformation (
327
). Then, we obtain

g\cdot S(T^{\pm})\cdot V_{0}^{\pm}\cdot S(T^{\pm})^{-1}\cdot g=\Lambda^{\pm}

(380)

6.6
Discussion of the Partition Function

We consider the partition function

Z=\sum_{i=1}^{2n}\lambda_{i}^{m}\,.

(381)

It is clear from the formulation of the partition function that as
m
increases, the contribution coming from the biggest eigenvalue
\lambda_{max}
will increase exponentially. In fact, for sufficiently large
m
,

Z\sim f\cdot\lambda_{max}^{m}\,,

(382)

where
f
is the degeneracy of
\lambda_{max}
. Thus, it is important to examine the ordering of the eigenvalues
\lambda_{k}
.
We have two sets of eigenvalues
\lambda^{\pm}
, such that

\displaystyle\ln\lambda^{+}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\pm\gamma_{1}\pm\gamma_{3}\pm\gamma_{5}\pm\cdots\pm\gamma_{2n-1})

(383)

\displaystyle\ln\lambda^{-}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\pm\gamma_{0}\pm\gamma_{2}\pm\gamma_{4}\pm\cdots\pm\gamma_{2n-2})

(384)

with an even number of minus signs at each combination, as explained in section
6.4
. Obviously, the biggest eigenvalues in each set will be the ones with no minus signs since we defined
\gamma_{k}
to be non-negative.

\displaystyle\lambda^{+}_{max}

\displaystyle=\exp{\frac{1}{2}(\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{3}+\gamma_{5}+\cdots+\gamma_{2n-1})}

(385)

\displaystyle\lambda^{-}_{max}

\displaystyle=\exp{\frac{1}{2}(\gamma_{0}+\gamma_{2}+\gamma_{4}+\cdots+\gamma_{2n-2})}

(386)

For large
n
, it follows from their definition that
\gamma_{2r}=\gamma_{2r-1}
, hence
\lambda^{+}_{max}
and
\lambda^{-}_{max}
are almost equal for low temperatures up to the critical point. However,
\gamma_{0}
falls of very rapidly relative to
\gamma_{1}
, thus
\lambda^{-}_{max}
becomes negligible compared to
\lambda^{+}_{max}
for temperatures higher than the critical point. This can be seen clearly in Fig.
57
.

Figure 57
:
\gamma_{k}
graph.

Let us look at the case where the interaction energy is the same for the row and column interactions, namely the case of a quadratic crystal that is, the case
J=J^{\prime}
and therefore
H=H^{\prime}
. Thus, from eq.
370
we have

\cosh{\gamma_{k}}=\cosh{2\bar{b}}\cdot\cosh{2b}-\sinh{2b}\cdot\sinh{2\bar{b}}\cos{\frac{\pi k}{n}}\,.

(387)

From the definition of
b
and
\bar{b}
, it follows that

\cosh{\gamma_{k}}=\coth{2\bar{b}}\cdot\cosh{2b}-\cos{\frac{\pi k}{n}}\,.

(388)

We can deduce the value of the critical point
H_{c}
using this formulation. Every
\gamma_{k}
should have a minimum at the critical point, hence

\coth{2H_{c}}\cdot\sinh{2H_{c}}-\frac{\cosh{2H_{c}}}{\sinh^{2}{2H_{c}}}=0\,,

(389)

thus
H_{c}
satisfies

\sinh^{2}{2H_{c}}=1

(390)

With a simple calculation,

H_{c}\approx 0.4406867935\cdots

(391)

We have said that
\gamma_{0}
acts differently from the rest of
\gamma_{k}
’s. For
\gamma_{0}
we have

\cosh{\gamma_{0}}=\cosh{2\bar{b}}\cdot\cosh{2b}-\sinh{2\bar{b}}\cdot\sinh{2b}\cos{\frac{0\cdot\pi}{n}}=\cosh{2(\bar{b}-b)}\,.

(392)

As it can also been seen from Fig.
57
,
\gamma_{0}
changes sign at the critical point. The other
\gamma_{k}
’s are almost symmetric about the
H_{c}
point. This causes
\lambda_{max}^{-}
to increase rapidly compared to
\lambda_{max}^{+}
. Thus

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda_{max}^{-}}{\lambda_{max}^{+}}=\begin{cases}1&\text{for }b>H_{c}\text{ and }T<T_{c}\,,\\
e^{2(b-\bar{b}}&\text{for }b<H_{c}\text{ and }T>T_{c}\,.\end{cases}

When
b>H_{c}
,

2\sinh{2b}^{-mn/2}\cdot Z\sim 2\lambda_{max}=2\exp{\frac{m}{2}(\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{3}+\cdots+\gamma_{2n-1})}\,,

(393)

and when
b<H_{c}
,

\sinh{2b}^{-mn/2}\cdot Z\sim\lambda_{max}=2\exp{\frac{m}{2}(\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{3}+\cdots+\gamma_{2n-1})}\,.

(394)

The exact partition function does not differ much from the approximate result. For the exact solution, we have

Z=(\sinh{2b})^{mn/2}\cdot\left[\prod_{k=1}^{n}\left(2\cosh{\frac{m}{2}\gamma_{2k-1}}\right)+\left(2\sinh{\frac{m}{2}\gamma_{2k-1}}\right)+\prod_{k=1}^{n}\left(2\cosh{\frac{m}{2}\gamma_{2k}}\right)+\left(2\sinh{\frac{m}{2}\gamma_{2k}}\right)\right]\,.

(395)

We can take
\gamma_{2r}=\gamma_{2r-1}
where
r=1,\cdots,n-1
, for large
n
. We must point out that the case is different for
\gamma_{0}
,

\gamma_{0}=\begin{cases}\gamma_{1}&\text{for }T<T_{c}\,,\\
-\gamma_{1}&\text{for }T>T_{c}\,.\end{cases}

Therefore, the two terms with products of
\cosh
in eq.
395

are equal regardless of the critical point whereas the terms with products are equal for
T<T_{c}
and cancel each other for
T\geq T_{c}
. Hence

\displaystyle(2\sinh{2b})^{-mn/2}\cdot Z\cong\begin{cases}&\prod_{k=1}^{n}\left(2\cosh{\frac{m}{2}\gamma_{2k-1}}\right)\text{for }T\geq T_{c}\,,\\
&\prod_{k=1}^{n}\left(2\cosh{\frac{m}{2}\gamma_{2k-1}}\right)+\left(2\sinh{\frac{m}{2}\gamma_{2k-1}}\right)\\
&=\prod_{k=1}^{n}\left(2\cosh{\frac{m}{2}\gamma_{2k-1}}\right)\cdot\left[1+\prod_{k=1}^{n}\left(\tanh{\frac{m}{2}\gamma_{2k-1}}\right)\right]\text{for }T<T_{c}\,.\end{cases}

(396)

Since
m
is very large,

2\cosh{\frac{m}{2}\gamma_{k}}\sim\exp{\frac{m}{2}\gamma_{k}}

with an exception only if
T
is in a close neighbourhood of
T_{c}
. So we can write

(2\sinh{2b})^{-mn/2}\cdot Z\sim\exp{\frac{m}{2}(\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{3}+\cdots+\gamma_{2n-1})}\equiv\lambda_{max}^{m}\text{ for }T>T_{c}

(397)

and

(2\sinh{2b})^{-mn/2}\cdot Z\sim\eta\cdot\lambda_{max}^{m}\text{ for }T<T_{c}\,,

(398)

where

\eta=\left[1+\prod_{k=1}^{n}\left(\tanh{\frac{m}{2}\gamma_{2k-1}}\right)\right]\,.

We remark that the product of factors
\tanh{\frac{m}{2}\gamma_{k}}
is never larger than
1
, thus
1<\eta<2
. For large
m
,
\eta\sim 2
rather close to
T_{c}
. The appearance of
\eta
here is equivalent to the two-fold degeneracy of
\lambda_{max}
for
T<T_{c}
in the approximation solution of
Z
.

7
Simplifying Kaufman’s Solution
(Cansu Ozdemir and Sinan Ulaş Öztürk)

We review
[
18
]
. Our aim is to find the partition function of the two-dimensional Ising model on an
m\times n
square lattice with zero magnetic field and toroidal boundary conditions by expressing the transfer matrix in terms of two commuting representations for the generators of
SO(2n,\mathbb{C})
algebra. After that, we diagonalize the transfer matrix by finding its eigenvectors and eigenvalues.

7.1
The Model and its Transfer Matrix

The Energy for the two-dimensional Ising model with
m
rows and
n
columns given a particular microstate
\{s_{\mu\nu}\}
and
J_{a}
as interaction energy parameter between rows and
J_{b}
between columns is given by,

E=J_{a}\sum^{m}_{\mu=1}\sum_{\nu=1}^{n}s_{\mu\nu}s_{\mu+1,\nu}+J_{b}\sum^{m}_{\mu=1}\sum_{\nu=1}^{n}s_{\mu\nu}s_{\mu,\nu+1}

Then the partition function is as follows,

Z(a,b)=\sum_{\{s\}}\exp\left(a\sum^{m}_{\mu=1}\sum_{\nu=1}^{n}s_{\mu\nu}s_{\mu+1,\nu}+b\sum^{m}_{\mu=1}\sum_{\nu=1}^{n}s_{\mu\nu}s_{\mu,\nu+1}\right)=

\sum_{\{s\}}\prod_{\mu=1}^{m}\left(\prod_{\nu=1}^{n}\exp(as_{\mu\nu}s_{\mu+1,\nu})\exp(bs_{\mu\nu}s_{\mu,\nu+1})\right)=\sum_{\{s\}}\prod_{\mu=1}^{m}\langle\pi_{\mu}|T|\pi_{\mu+1}\rangle

=\textrm{Tr}T^{m}

Where
T
is called
the transfer matrix
and
a=-\beta J_{a}
,
b=-\beta J_{b}
. Elements of the transfer matrix are given by

\langle\pi_{\mu}|T|\pi_{\mu+1}\rangle=\prod_{\nu=1}^{n}\exp(as_{\mu\nu}s_{\mu+1,\nu})\exp(bs_{\mu\nu}s_{\mu,\nu+1})

7.1.1
Matrix Elements of
T

To be able to see how
|\pi_{\mu}\rangle
behaves and elements of
T
explicitly, let us explore the notion of ”all possible microstates” deeper. On a lattice site of size
(m,n)
there are
m
rows and
n
columns. Then in a given row, there are
n
spin sites. If we find a way to list all of the possible values that the spin sites for a given row can take, then we can index each of these microstates, say with the letter
i
, and indicate the value of any spin at
\nu
th place of a row in
i
th microstate as
s_{\nu}^{(i)}=\pm 1
.

One way to do that is to switch the sign of the
\nu
th spin in every
i=2^{n-\nu}
value. For example for
n=4
, The table below is the way to list all
2^{4}=16
microstates,

++++\quad i=1

-+++\quad i=9

+++-\quad i=2

-++-\quad i=10

++-+\quad i=3

-+-+\quad i=11

++--\quad i=4

-+--\quad i=12

+-++\quad i=5

--++\quad i=13

+-+-\quad i=6

--+-\quad i=14

+--+\quad i=7

---+\quad i=15

+---\quad i=8

----\quad i=16

Where
+
and
-
are short for
+1
and
-1
. What we are doing is representing these spin sites as mod 2 numbers where 0 represents
+
and 1 represents
-
. Let
\chi(m)
be a function that is defined to be
-1
when
m
is even and
+1
when
m
is odd. That is,

\displaystyle\chi(m)=\begin{cases}-1,\quad\textrm{$m$ is even}\\
+1,\quad\textrm{$m$ is odd}\end{cases}

So, in essence, the function
\chi
plays the role of taking modulo 2. Then the value of
\nu
th spin in the
i
th configuration is given by

s_{\nu}^{(i)}=\chi\left(\left\lceil\dfrac{i}{2^{n-\nu}}\right\rceil\right)

These
i
values which represent spin configurations can be viewed as an ordering for the elements of the following set

S(n)=\{+,-\}^{n}=\underbrace{\{+,-\}\times\{+,-\}\dots\times\{+,-\}}_{n\textrm{ times}}

Let us name these elements
r_{i}
such that
r_{i}\in S(n)
and
r_{i}
is the
i
th element of
S(n)
. For example for
n=4
,
r_{12}
is the following ,

r_{12}=(-,+,-,-)=-+--

Finally, let us define a vector
|r\rangle_{n}=|r\rangle
for any given
n
. We omit
n
for brevity.

|r\rangle=\sum_{i}r_{i}|i\rangle

In this representation, we can consider vectors
|i\rangle
as standard basis vectors. Hence,

|r\rangle=\underbrace{(++\dots+)}_{r_{1}}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\\
0\\
\vdots\\
0\end{bmatrix}+\underbrace{(++\dots+-)}_{r_{2}}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\\
0\\
\vdots\\
0\end{bmatrix}+\dots+\underbrace{(--\dots-)}_{r_{2^{n}}}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
0\\
\vdots\\
0\\
1\end{bmatrix}

If we represent spin states by vectors, as follows,

\displaystyle|s^{(i)}_{\nu}\rangle=\begin{cases}|+\rangle=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}\quad\textrm{ if $s^{(i)}_{\nu}$}=+1\\
\\
|-\rangle=\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}\quad\textrm{ if $s^{(i)}_{\nu}$}=-1\end{cases}

Using these vectors we can construct
2^{n}
number of vectors in the standard basis by taking the Kronecker product of spin vectors.

|i\rangle=\bigotimes_{\nu=1}^{n}|s^{(i)}_{\nu}\rangle

(399)

To give the same example as above, for
n=4
we can construct
|12\rangle
as below,

|12\rangle=|-\rangle\otimes|+\rangle\otimes|-\rangle\otimes|-\rangle=\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}\otimes\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}\otimes\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}\otimes\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}

Let us get back to calculating the partition function. Imagine there are two rows, i.e.
\mu=1,2
. Then partition function would take the form,

Z=\sum_{i,j}\exp\left(\sum_{\nu}as^{(i)}_{\nu}s^{(j)}_{\nu}\right)\exp(\sum_{\nu}bs^{(j)}_{\nu}s^{(j)}_{\nu+1})\exp(\sum_{\nu}bs^{(i)}_{\nu}s^{(i)}_{\nu+1})=\sum_{i,j}V^{a}_{ij}V^{b}_{i}V^{b}_{j}

Where
i
represents a sum over all the possible states of the first row (
\mu=1
) and
j
represents the same thing for the second row (
\mu=2
). The contribution due to Interaction between adjacent columns
V^{b}
, depends only on the state of the row that makes the contribution so it depends only on either
i
or
j
. If we generalize this for
m
rows, that is
\mu=1,\dots m
, we get,

Z=\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots i_{m}}V^{a}_{i_{1}i_{2}}V^{a}_{i_{2}i_{3}}\dots V^{a}_{i_{m}i_{1}}V^{b}_{i_{1}}\dots V^{b}_{i_{m}}=\sum_{i_{1}\dots i_{m}}\prod_{\mu}V^{a}_{i_{\mu}i_{\mu+1}}V^{b}_{i_{\mu}}

Where we have toroidal boundary conditions
i_{m+1}=i_{1}
. Here,

\displaystyle\begin{cases}V^{a}_{ij}=\exp(a\sum_{\nu}s_{\nu}^{(i)}s_{\nu}^{(j)})=\prod_{\nu}\exp(as_{\nu}^{(i)}s_{\nu}^{(j)})\\
\\
V_{i}^{b}=\prod_{\nu}\exp(bs^{(i)}_{\nu}s^{(i)}_{\nu+1})\end{cases}

(400)

Let us slightly alter the representation of
V_{i}^{b}
to look like matrix elements.

V_{ij}^{b}=\delta_{ij}\prod_{\nu}\exp(bs_{\nu}^{(i)}s^{(j)}_{\nu+1})

Now we can regard
V^{a}_{ij}
and
V^{b}_{ij}
as matrices. Then we can think of their matrix elements in terms of basis vectors,

V^{a,b}_{ij}=\langle i|V^{a,b}|j\rangle

If we define
T=V^{a}V^{b}
, elements of
T
are

T_{ij}=\sum_{k}V^{a}_{ik}V^{b}_{kj}=\sum_{k}\prod_{\nu}\exp(as^{(i)}_{\nu}s^{(k)}_{\nu})\exp(bs^{(k)}_{\nu}s^{(j)}_{\nu+1})\delta_{kj}=\prod_{\nu}\exp(as^{(i)}_{\nu}s^{(j)}_{\nu}+bs^{(j)}_{\nu}s^{(j)}_{\nu+1})

Which is exactly the summand in the partition function.
T
is called
transfer matrix.
From here, we can rewrite the partition function,

Z=\sum_{i_{1}}\sum_{i_{2}\dots i_{m}}\prod_{\mu}\langle i_{\mu}|T|i_{\mu+1}\rangle=\sum_{i_{1}}\langle i_{1}|T^{m}|i_{1}\rangle=\Tr T^{m}\,.

It is given in the article that the matrices
V^{a}
and
V^{b}
can be expressed using newly defined matrices
X_{\nu}
,
Y_{\nu}
and
Z_{\nu}
in terms of Pauli matrices. Let us state Pauli matrices,

\sigma_{x}=\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix},\quad\sigma_{y}=\begin{bmatrix}0&-i\\
i&0\end{bmatrix},\quad\sigma_{z}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{bmatrix}

Then define

X_{\nu}=\underbrace{\mathds{1}\otimes\cdots\mathds{1}\otimes\sigma_{x}}_{\textrm{$\nu$th place}}\otimes\mathds{1}\dots\otimes\mathds{1}=\bigotimes_{k=1}^{\nu-1}\mathds{1}\otimes\sigma_{x}\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{k=\nu+1}^{n}\mathds{1}\right)\,.

(401)

Y_{\nu}
,
Z_{\nu}
are defined in the same way by replacing
\sigma_{x}
by
\sigma_{y}
and
\sigma_{z}
respectively. Then, further define
\bar{a}>0
as by
\sinh 2\bar{a}\sinh 2a=1
and say,

V^{a}=(2\sinh 2a)^{n/2}\prod_{\nu=1}^{n}\exp(\bar{a}X_{\nu})

Notice,

\tanh a=e^{-2\bar{a}}\quad;\quad\tanh\bar{a}=e^{-2a}

and
V^{b}
is given by

V^{b}=\prod_{\nu}\exp(bZ_{\nu}Z_{\nu+1})

Let us investigate these matrices separately and why these products of matrices are equal to counting interaction energies for rows and columns separately.

7.1.2
Elements of
V^{a}

Our aim now is to investigate and understand the relationship between the given product

\langle i|V^{a}|j\rangle=(2\sinh 2a)^{n/2}\langle i|\prod_{\nu=1}^{n}\exp(\bar{a}X_{\nu})|j\rangle

(402)

and the contribution to the partition function given in terms of different configurations and spin values,

\langle i|V^{a}|j\rangle=\prod_{\nu}\exp(as_{\nu}^{(i)}s_{\nu}^{(j)})

(403)

To see this connection, let us first look at how
X_{\nu}
acts on a state
|i\rangle
. Any spin is either
+1
or
-1
thus,
|s_{\nu}^{(i)}\rangle=|\pm\rangle
. If we act on
|s_{\nu}^{(i)}\rangle
we get,

\sigma_{x}|+\rangle=\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}=|-\rangle

Similarly,

\sigma_{x}|-\rangle=|+\rangle

We can write these two equations in one expression,

\sigma_{x}|s^{(i)}_{\nu}\rangle=|-s^{(i)}_{\nu}\rangle

(404)

Since
|i\rangle
can be written in terms of
|s_{\nu}^{(i)}\rangle
by the (
399
), we have,

X_{\nu}|i\rangle=\left[\bigotimes_{k=1}^{\nu-1}\mathds{1}\otimes\sigma_{x}\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{k=\nu+1}^{n}\mathds{1}\right)\right]\bigotimes_{\lambda=1}^{n}|s^{(i)}_{\lambda}\rangle=\bigotimes_{\lambda=1}^{\nu-1}|s^{(i)}_{\lambda}\rangle\otimes\sigma_{x}|s^{(i)}_{\nu}\rangle\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{\lambda=\nu+1}^{n}|s^{(i)}_{\lambda}\rangle\right)

=\bigotimes_{\lambda=1}^{\nu-1}|s^{(i)}_{\lambda}\rangle\otimes|-s^{(i)}_{\nu}\rangle\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{\lambda=\nu+1}^{n}|s^{(i)}_{\lambda}\rangle\right)

Which corresponds to another state. In fact, this is the state where the sign of the
\nu
th spin site is flipped! This is very nice since what
\langle i|V^{a}|j\rangle
actually does is to compare each spin site on the
\nu
th position for the
|i\rangle
and
|j\rangle
states. If the spin sites have different signs, multiply by
e^{-a}
and if they are the same, multiply by
e^{a}
for all
\nu\in\{1,2\dots n\}
.
X_{\nu}
allows us to flip the
\nu
th spin site for a given configuration. Let us have a look at this new configuration which is also an element of
S(n)
. It is not obvious to see what will be the order of this new state in relation to
i
.
X_{\nu}
will take
|i\rangle
where
i\in\{1,2,\dots,2^{n}\}
and give another element in the same set as an output. This is a kind of permutation. Let us denote this permutation by
P_{\nu}
thus,

X_{\nu}|i\rangle=|P_{\nu}(i)\rangle

(405)

Where,

P_{\nu}:\{1,\dots 2^{n}\}\to\{1,\dots 2^{n}\}\quad;\quad P_{\nu}\in\textrm{Sym}(2^{n})

It is not necessary to explicitly show what
P_{\nu}
is. Nevertheless, in this equation, we can give an expression for
P_{\nu}
which is,

P_{\nu}(i)=i+\chi\left(\left\lceil\dfrac{i}{2^{n-\nu}}\right\rceil\right)2^{n-\nu}

Now Let us expand the (
402
),

\exp(\bar{a}X_{\nu})=\sum_{k}\dfrac{(\bar{a}X_{\nu})^{k}}{k!}

Notice that
X_{\nu}^{2}=\mathds{1}_{2^{n}}
since
\sigma_{x}^{2}=\mathds{1}_{2}
. Thus,

\exp(\bar{a}X_{\nu})=\cosh\bar{a}\mathds{1}+\sinh\bar{a}X_{\nu}=\cosh\bar{a}(\mathds{1}+\tanh\bar{a}X_{\nu})=\cosh\bar{a}(\mathds{1}+e^{-2a}X_{\nu})

From here,

\prod_{\nu}\exp(\bar{a}X_{\nu})=\prod_{\nu}\cosh\bar{a}(\mathds{1}+e^{-2a}X_{\nu})=\cosh^{n}\bar{a}\prod_{\nu}(\mathds{1}+e^{-2a}X_{\nu})

Then,
V_{a}
is,

V^{a}=(2\sinh 2a)^{n/2}\cosh^{n}\bar{a}\prod_{\nu}(\mathds{1}+e^{-2a}X_{\nu})

The coefficient of this expression is,

\sqrt{2\sinh 2a}\cosh\bar{a}=\dfrac{\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{\sinh 2\bar{a}}}\cosh\bar{a}=\sqrt{\dfrac{2\cosh^{2}\bar{a}}{2\sinh\bar{a}\cosh\bar{a}}}=\sqrt{\dfrac{\cosh\bar{a}}{\sinh\bar{a}}}=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{\tanh\bar{a}}}=e^{a}

Thus we arrive at the final expression,

V^{a}=e^{na}\prod_{\nu}(\mathds{1}+e^{-2a}X_{\nu})

(406)

This is just another expression for (
402
). We can already see how this expression will count all spin sites. If we expand this product we see the explicit terms that lead to a transition between
{\left|{i}\right>}
and
{\left|{j}\right>}
,

e^{na}\left(\mathds{1}+e^{-2a}\sum_{\nu}X_{\nu}+e^{-4a}\sum_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2};\nu_{1}<\nu_{2}}X_{\nu_{1}}X_{\nu_{2}}+\dots+e^{-2na}\prod_{\nu}X_{\nu}\right)

The first term corresponds to
{\left|{i}\right>}
and
{\left|{j}\right>}
configurations being the same. Then all the energy terms will add up to
e^{na}
since each
\nu
will contribute
e^{a}
to the final product. If however,
{\left|{i}\right>}
and
{\left|{j}\right>}
differ only by one spin site, say the spin at
\nu^{*}
, then
V^{a}_{ij}
will be
e^{(n-2)a}
. Also notice,
P_{\nu^{*}}{\left|{i}\right>}={\left|{j}\right>}
. Hence every flip in the configuration corresponds to a
e^{-2a}
difference from
e^{na}
. With this information in mind, we can interpret the matrix elements,

{\left<{i}\right|}V^{a}{\left|{j}\right>}=e^{na}\left(\langle i|j\rangle+e^{-2a}\sum_{\nu}{\left<{i}\right|}X_{\nu}{\left|{j}\right>}+\dots+e^{-2na}{\left<{i}\right|}\prod_{\nu}X_{\nu}{\left|{j}\right>}\right)

=e^{na}\left(\delta_{ij}+e^{-2a}\sum_{\nu}\delta_{i,P_{\nu}(j)}+\dots+e^{-2na}\delta_{i,P_{1}\dots P_{n}(j)}\right)

7.1.3
Elements of
V^{b}

As for
V^{b}
, we have a very similar expression with
V^{a}
. However, its interpretation will be different. Expression for
V^{b}
is given by,

{\left<{i}\right|}V^{b}{\left|{j}\right>}=\delta_{ij}\prod_{\nu}\exp(bs^{(i)}_{\nu}s^{(j)}_{\nu+1})

(407)

Which is said to be equal to,

{\left<{i}\right|}V^{b}{\left|{j}\right>}={\left<{i}\right|}\prod_{\nu}\exp(bZ_{\nu}Z_{\nu+1}){\left|{j}\right>}

(408)

let us first observe how
Z_{\nu}
affects a basis vector
{\left|{i}\right>}
. Just as above,

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\sigma_{z}{\left|{+}\right>}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}={\left|{+}\right>}\\
\\
\sigma_{z}{\left|{-}\right>}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}0\\
-1\end{bmatrix}=-{\left|{-}\right>}\end{cases}

Which is in short,

\sigma_{z}{\left|{s^{(i)}_{\nu}}\right>}=s^{(i)}_{\nu}{\left|{s^{(i)}_{\nu}}\right>}

(409)

Then, using (
399
),

Z_{\nu}{\left|{i}\right>}=\left[\bigotimes_{k=1}^{\nu-1}\mathds{1}\otimes\sigma_{z}\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{k=\nu+1}^{n}\mathds{1}\right)\right]\bigotimes_{\lambda=1}^{n}|s^{(i)}_{\lambda}\rangle=\bigotimes_{\lambda=1}^{\nu-1}|s^{(i)}_{\lambda}\rangle\otimes\sigma_{z}|s^{(i)}_{\nu}\rangle\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{\lambda=\nu+1}^{n}|s^{(i)}_{\lambda}\rangle\right)

=\bigotimes_{\lambda=1}^{\nu-1}|s^{(i)}_{\lambda}\rangle\otimes s^{(i)}_{\nu}|s^{(i)}_{\nu}\rangle\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{\lambda=\nu+1}^{n}|s^{(i)}_{\lambda}\rangle\right)=s^{(i)}_{\nu}{\left|{i}\right>}

Thus, the effect of
Z_{\nu}
on a basis vector is multiplying the same vector by
\nu
th spin value. i.e.,

Z_{\nu}{\left|{i}\right>}=s^{(i)}_{\nu}{\left|{i}\right>}

(410)

From here, we have

Z_{\nu}Z_{\nu+1}{\left|{i}\right>}=s^{(i)}_{\nu}s^{(i)}_{\nu+1}{\left|{i}\right>}

At this point, let us introduce
W_{\nu}=Z_{\nu}Z_{\nu+1}
and
w_{\nu}=s^{(i)}_{\nu}s^{(i)}_{\nu+1}
for brevity. The equation above becomes

W_{\nu}{\left|{i}\right>}=w_{\nu}{\left|{i}\right>}

Where
w_{\nu}
takes the values
\pm 1
.
w_{n}
is essentially an indicator to tell if
\nu
th and
(\nu+1)
th spin sites have the same value.
w_{\nu}
is
+1
when the
\nu
th spin site and
(\nu+1)
th spin site has the same sign. It equals
-1
when they have different signs.
{\left|{i}\right>}
is an eigenvector of
W_{\nu}
with eigenvalue
w_{n}
. Then we have the following relation,

\exp(bW_{\nu}){\left|{i}\right>}=\exp(bw_{\nu}){\left|{i}\right>}\Rightarrow\prod_{\nu}\exp(bW_{\nu}){\left|{i}\right>}=\prod_{\nu}\exp(bw_{\nu}){\left|{i}\right>}

Finally, we have,

{\left<{i}\right|}V^{b}{\left|{j}\right>}={\left<{i}\right|}\prod_{\nu}\exp(bW_{\nu}){\left|{j}\right>}=\prod_{\nu}\exp(bw_{\nu}){\left<{i}\right|}{\left|{j}\right>}=\delta_{ij}\prod_{\nu}\exp(bw_{\nu})

Plugging in the expressions for
W_{\nu}
and
w_{\nu}
we get,

{\left<{i}\right|}\prod_{\nu}\exp(bZ_{\nu}Z_{\nu+1}){\left|{j}\right>}=\delta_{ij}\prod_{\nu}\exp(bs^{(i)}_{\nu}s^{(j)}_{\nu+1})

(411)

7.2
\Gamma_{\nu}
Matrices and Algebras of Their Projected Bilinears

Spin representations of
2^{n}
-dimensional rotation groups can be obtained by defining
2n
matrices with a basis for the
2\times 2
matrix algebra. For convenience, let us choose the basis as the Pauli matrices with the identity. It is noteworthy that the basis can be chosen arbitrarily, and there always exist transformations between two arbitrary bases. For a detailed discussion, see
[
22
]
and chapter 10 of
[
23
]
. Let us define
2n
matrices such that

\displaystyle\Gamma_{2\nu-1}

\displaystyle=X_{1}\cdots X_{\nu-1}\cdot Z_{\nu}=\bigotimes_{k=1}^{\nu-1}\sigma_{X}\otimes\sigma_{Z}\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{k=\nu+1}^{n}\mathds{1}\right)

(412)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{2\nu}

\displaystyle=X_{1}\cdots X_{\nu-1}\cdot Y_{\nu}=\bigotimes_{k=1}^{\nu-1}\sigma_{X}\otimes\sigma_{Y}\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{k=\nu+1}^{n}\mathds{1}\right)

(413)

The defined matrices obey
\{\Gamma_{\mu},\Gamma_{\nu}\}=2\delta_{\mu\nu}
. Let us define a matrix that is the product of all
X_{i}
matrices. This matrix can be expressed using can be expressed using
\Gamma_{\nu}
matrices.

\displaystyle U_{X}=X_{1}\cdots X_{n}=i^{n}\Gamma_{1}\cdots\Gamma_{2n}

(414)

Using the properties of the spin matrices, this equation can be explicitly written as

\displaystyle i^{n}\Gamma_{1}\cdots\Gamma_{2n}

\displaystyle=i^{n}Z_{1}Y_{1}X_{1}Z_{2}X_{1}Y_{2}\cdots X_{1}\cdots X_{n-1}Z_{n}X_{1}\cdots X_{n-1}Y_{n}

(415)

\displaystyle=i^{n}Z_{1}Y_{1}Z_{2}Y_{2}\cdots Z_{n}Y_{n}

(416)

\displaystyle=i^{n}(-i)X_{1}(-i)X_{2}\cdots(-i)X_{n}=X_{1}\cdots X_{n}.

(417)

By the properties of the spin matrices, it is clear that
U_{X}^{2}=X_{1}^{2}\cdots X_{n}^{2}=1
. The
U_{X}
matrix has the property of anti-commuting with every
\Gamma_{\mu}
,

\displaystyle\{\Gamma_{\mu},U_{X}\}

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{\mu}U_{X}+U_{X}\Gamma_{\mu}

(418)

\displaystyle=(X_{1}\cdots X_{\mu-1}T_{\mu}I_{\mu+1}\cdots I_{n})(X_{1}\cdots X_{n})+(X_{1}\cdots X_{\mu-1}T_{\mu}I_{\mu+1}\cdots I_{n})(X_{1}\cdots X_{n})

(419)

\displaystyle=\{T_{\mu},X_{\mu}\}=0

(420)

Where
T=Z
or
T=Y
. Using only
U_{X}
and bilinears
\Gamma_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\beta}
we can express
X_{\nu}
and
Z_{\nu}Z_{\nu+1}

which were previously used in the formulation of
V
.

\displaystyle X_{\nu}

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{2}[\Gamma_{2\nu},\Gamma_{2\nu-1}]

(421)

\displaystyle Z_{\nu}Z_{\nu+1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{2}[\Gamma_{2\nu+1},\Gamma_{2\nu}]

(422)

\displaystyle Z_{n}Z_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{2}U_{X}[\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2n}]

(423)

These relations will lead to two commuting algebras of projected bilinears of the
\Gamma_{\mu}
.

Let us define the projectors

P^{\pm}\equiv\frac{1}{2}(1\pm U_{X})

(424)

which has the properties

\displaystyle P^{+}+P^{-}=1=\frac{1}{2}(2+U_{X}-U_{X})

(425)

\displaystyle P^{\pm}P^{\pm}=P^{\pm}=\frac{1}{4}(1\pm 2U_{X}+U_{X}^{2})=\frac{1}{2}(1\pm U_{X})

(426)

\displaystyle P^{+}P^{-}=0=P^{-}P^{+}=\frac{1}{4}(1\pm U_{X}\mp U_{X}-U_{X}^{2})

(427)

\displaystyle P^{\pm}U_{X}=U_{X}P^{\pm}=\pm P^{\pm}=\frac{1}{2}(1\pm U_{X})U_{X}=\frac{1}{2}(U_{X}\pm 1)=\pm\frac{1}{2}(1\pm U_{X})

(428)

And satisfy

\displaystyle[P^{\pm},\Gamma_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\beta}]=P^{\pm}\Gamma_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\beta}-\Gamma_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\beta}P^{\pm}

(429)

\displaystyle=(\frac{1}{2}(1\pm U_{X})\Gamma_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\beta})-(\frac{1}{2}\Gamma_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\beta}(1\pm U_{X}))

(430)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\Gamma_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\beta}\pm U_{X}\Gamma_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\beta}-\Gamma_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\beta}\mp\Gamma_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\beta}U_{X})

(431)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\pm U_{X}\Gamma_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\beta}\mp\Gamma_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\beta}U_{X})

(432)

We know that
\{\Gamma_{\mu},U_{X}\}=0
, thus
\Gamma_{\mu}U_{X}=-U_{X}\Gamma_{\mu}
. Hence,

\displaystyle[P^{\pm},\Gamma_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\beta}]=\frac{1}{2}(\pm\Gamma_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\beta}U_{X}\mp\Gamma_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\beta}U_{X})=0

(433)

With these matrices, define

\displaystyle J_{\alpha\beta}=-\frac{i}{4}[\Gamma_{\alpha},\Gamma_{\beta}]

(434)

\displaystyle J_{\alpha\beta}^{\pm}=P^{\pm}J_{\alpha\beta}\,.

(435)

Notice that

\displaystyle J_{\alpha\beta}=P^{+}J_{\alpha\beta}+P^{-}J_{\alpha\beta}=\frac{1}{2}(1+U_{X})(-\frac{i}{4}[\Gamma_{\alpha},\Gamma_{\beta}])+\frac{1}{2}(1-U_{X})(-\frac{i}{4}[\Gamma_{\alpha},\Gamma_{\beta}])=J_{\alpha\beta}^{+}+J_{\alpha\beta}^{-}

\displaystyle U_{X}J_{\alpha\beta}^{\pm}=(U_{X}P^{\pm})J_{\alpha\beta}=\pm P^{\pm}J_{\alpha\beta}^{\pm}=\pm P^{\pm}J_{\alpha\beta}^{\pm}

Clearly,
J_{\alpha\beta}^{\pm}=-\frac{i}{4}[\Gamma_{\alpha},\Gamma_{\beta}]=\frac{i}{4}[\Gamma_{\beta},\Gamma_{\alpha}]=-J_{\beta\alpha}^{\pm}
.
Now let us discuss the number of independent
J_{\alpha\beta}^{\pm}
matrices there are. Remark that, since
\{\Gamma_{\mu},\Gamma_{\nu}\}=2\delta_{\mu\nu}
, for
\mu\neq\nu
,
\Gamma_{\mu}\Gamma_{\nu}=-\Gamma_{\nu}\Gamma_{\mu}
, hence for each
\nu
, there are
(2n-1)

\Gamma_{\mu}
matrices such that
J_{\nu\mu}\neq 0
. Thus, there are a total of
n(2n-1)
independent matrices of each type
J_{\alpha\beta}^{\pm}
. Their algebra decomposes into two commuting parts

\displaystyle[J_{\alpha\beta}^{+},J_{\gamma\delta}^{-}]

(436)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(1+U_{X})(-\frac{i}{4}[\Gamma_{\alpha},\Gamma_{\beta}])\cdot\frac{1}{2}(1-U_{X})(-\frac{i}{4}[\Gamma_{\gamma},\Gamma_{\delta}])

(437)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{-i}{4}\right)^{2}(([\Gamma_{\alpha},\Gamma_{\beta}]+U_{X}[\Gamma_{\alpha},\Gamma_{\beta}])\cdot([\Gamma_{\gamma},\Gamma_{\delta}]-U_{X}[\Gamma_{\gamma},\Gamma_{\delta}])

(438)

\displaystyle-([\Gamma_{\gamma},\Gamma_{\delta}]-U_{X}[\Gamma_{\gamma},\Gamma_{\delta}])\cdot([\Gamma_{\alpha},\Gamma_{\beta}]+U_{X}[\Gamma_{\alpha},\Gamma_{\beta}]))

(439)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{-i}{4}\right)^{2}(([\Gamma_{\alpha},\Gamma_{\beta}][\Gamma_{\gamma},\Gamma_{\delta}]+U_{X}[\Gamma_{\alpha},\Gamma_{\beta}][\Gamma_{\gamma},\Gamma_{\delta}]-U_{X}[\Gamma_{\alpha},\Gamma_{\beta}][\Gamma_{\gamma},\Gamma_{\delta}]-[\Gamma_{\alpha},\Gamma_{\beta}][\Gamma_{\gamma},\Gamma_{\delta}])

(440)

\displaystyle-([\Gamma_{\gamma},\Gamma_{\delta}][\Gamma_{\alpha},\Gamma_{\beta}]-U_{X}[\Gamma_{\gamma},\Gamma_{\delta}][\Gamma_{\alpha},\Gamma_{\beta}]+U_{X}[\Gamma_{\gamma},\Gamma_{\delta}][\Gamma_{\alpha},\Gamma_{\beta}]-[\Gamma_{\gamma},\Gamma_{\delta}][\Gamma_{\alpha},\Gamma_{\beta}]))

(441)

\displaystyle=0

(442)

which obey the identical algebras

[J_{\alpha\beta}^{\pm},J_{\gamma\delta}^{\pm}]=i(\delta_{\alpha\gamma}J_{\beta\delta}^{\pm}+\delta_{\beta\delta}J_{\alpha\gamma}^{\pm}-\delta_{\alpha\delta}J_{\beta\gamma}^{\pm}-\delta_{\beta\gamma}J_{\alpha\delta}^{\pm})

(443)

This relation can be explicitly shown,

\displaystyle[J_{\alpha\beta}^{\pm},J_{\gamma\delta}^{\pm}]=(J_{\alpha\beta}^{\pm}J_{\gamma\delta}^{\pm}-J_{\gamma\delta}^{\pm}J_{\alpha\beta}^{\pm})

\displaystyle=(P^{\pm}J_{\alpha\beta}P^{\pm}J_{\gamma\delta}P^{\pm}J_{\alpha\beta})

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{-i}{4}\right)^{2}((P^{\pm}\Gamma_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\beta}-P^{\pm}\Gamma_{\beta}\Gamma_{\alpha})\cdot(P^{\pm}\Gamma_{\gamma}\Gamma_{\delta}-P^{\pm}\Gamma_{\delta}\Gamma_{\gamma}))

Since
[P^{\pm},\Gamma_{\mu}\Gamma_{\nu}]=0
and
P^{\pm}P^{\pm}=P^{\pm}
,

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{-i}{4}\right)^{2}P^{\pm}((\Gamma_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\beta}\Gamma_{\gamma}\Gamma_{\delta}-\Gamma_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\beta}\Gamma_{\delta}\Gamma_{\gamma}-\Gamma_{\beta}\Gamma_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\gamma}\Gamma_{\delta}+\Gamma_{\beta}\Gamma_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\delta}\Gamma_{\gamma})

(444)

\displaystyle-(\Gamma_{\gamma}\Gamma_{\delta}\Gamma_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\beta}-\Gamma_{\gamma}\Gamma_{\delta}\Gamma_{\beta}\Gamma_{\alpha}-\Gamma_{\delta}\Gamma_{\gamma}\Gamma_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\beta}+\Gamma_{\delta}\Gamma_{\gamma}\Gamma_{\beta}\Gamma_{\alpha}))

(445)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{-i}{4}\right)^{2}P^{\pm}((\Gamma_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\beta}\Gamma_{\gamma}\Gamma_{\delta}-\Gamma_{\delta}\Gamma_{\gamma}\Gamma_{\beta}\Gamma_{\alpha})+(\Gamma_{\delta}\Gamma_{\gamma}\Gamma_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\beta}-\Gamma_{\beta}\Gamma_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\gamma}\Gamma_{\delta})

(446)
